# PART XVII — TWO REAL FORMS OF ONE COMPLEX CANONICAL GEOMETRY: THE VERSION-22 BRANCH [T/V/D/S/O]

*Version 21 produced, locally, a structure more ambitious than it intended: the complexified canonical geometry carries two antiholomorphic involutions, the physical real structure $\rho$ and a derived second one, $\sigma = \widetilde\jmath\circ\rho$, whose fixed loci meet on the Galactic plane. This part suspends the hierarchy between them and tests the stronger thesis — that the primitive object is the complex canonical geometry carrying one compact real structure and one equally intrinsic split real-form *type*, $\mathcal R_{\rm split}\simeq\mathbb{RP}^2$, of which physical solutions select a member $\sigma_{[N]}$, the common fixed plane chosen after the axis rather than installed before it — as an experiment in architecture. Parts 0–XVI are unchanged and version 21.7 stands intact as the control; nothing here adds a field, a second connection, a second gravity or a rendering knob; the criterion for promotion is severe: the branch must derive more than it assumes, or it retires.*

## XVII.0 The thesis and the primitive datum [S/V]

Replace, provisionally, the real-first datum $(E,\rho_E)$ by the complex-first datum $(E_{\mathbb C},\hat g_{\mathbb C},\mathbb C^\times,\mathcal A_{\mathbb C},\rho,[\sigma],\widehat{\mathcal U}^{\mathbb C}_{\rm mer})$: the compact real structure $\rho$, and the *conjugacy class* $[\sigma]$ of split real structures — the isotropic family of §XVII.9, generated by the shell symmetry, of which a solution selects one member $\sigma$. With $\sigma$ so selected, $\jmath := \sigma\circ\rho$ is their holomorphic product. This is the economical formulation: not a family added as a new primitive field, and not an $\mathbb{RP}^2$-valued knob at every point, but one compact structure and one split *type*. On the local model of §XVI.8 the structure is verified rather than imposed: with $\rho$ the base conjugation and $w\mapsto\bar w^{-1}$ on the fiber, and $\widetilde\jmath$ the lifted plane reflection, $\sigma$ acts as $(z_\parallel,z_\perp,w)\mapsto(\bar z_\parallel,-\bar z_\perp,\bar w)$, is antiholomorphic, squares to one, and commutes with $\rho$, so $\{1,\rho,\sigma,\jmath\}\cong\mathbb Z_2\times\mathbb Z_2$ with $\jmath^2 = 1$ **[V]**. The two real forms are $E_\rho = \operatorname{Fix}\rho$, which must and does reproduce $E$, and $E_\sigma = \operatorname{Fix}\sigma$, new as an interpretation. "Equal footing" is a statement about *types*, not members: the compact real-form type, with its one reduction $U(1)$, and the split real-form type, occurring as an isotropic conjugacy family with axis-dependent real slices, are both intrinsic to the complex parent. It does not mean their real forms are isomorphic or interchangeable, and §XVII.8 keeps the inequivalence.

## XVII.1 Theorem A — the compact/split diamond [T/V]

On the complex fiber $\rho(w) = \bar w^{-1}$ and $\sigma(w) = \bar w$, so $\operatorname{Fix}\rho = U(1)$, $\operatorname{Fix}\sigma = \mathbb R^\times$, and $U(1)\cap\mathbb R^\times = \{\pm1\}$; on the Lie algebra $d\rho:\xi\mapsto-\bar\xi$ fixes $i\mathbb R$ and $d\sigma:\xi\mapsto\bar\xi$ fixes $\mathbb R$, with $i\mathbb R\cap\mathbb R = 0$ **[V]**. The identity components are the compact phase group and the noncompact scale group that Parts XV–XVI have been relating, now placed as the two real forms of one complex torus in a diamond, $\mathbb C^\times$ over $U(1)$ and $\mathbb R^\times$ over $\{\pm1\}$. The polar decomposition $\mathbb C^\times\simeq\mathbb R_+\times U(1)$ stays true and is deepened: **phase and scale are sibling real forms of one complex transport, the fixed groups of two distinct real structures.** The split form is not identified with charge: Part X's chiral rapidity remains a separate possible representation of the same group (§XVII.11).

## XVII.2 Theorem B — the bi-real connection, with an honest accounting [T/V/D]

Principal-bundle equivariance under an antiholomorphic involution whose fiber action induces $\xi\mapsto-\bar\xi$ or $\xi\mapsto\bar\xi$ gives the two reality conditions, $\rho^*\mathcal A_{\mathbb C} = -\overline{\mathcal A_{\mathbb C} }$ and $\sigma^*\mathcal A_{\mathbb C} = +\overline{\mathcal A_{\mathbb C} }$, and their product gives $\jmath^*\mathcal A_{\mathbb C} = -\mathcal A_{\mathbb C}$ — all three verified on the symmetric-gauge Hopf connection $\mathcal A_{\mathbb C} = -\tfrac i2\cos z_\theta\,dz_\phi$ **[V]**. The accounting is this: since $\sigma = \jmath\rho$, $\sigma$-reality given $\rho$-reality is *exactly* $\jmath$-equivariance. The brief hoped bi-reality would derive the oddness of the connection that version 21 left conditional; it does not — it assumes it under another name, and by this branch's own criterion that is neutral, not a gain. What bi-reality does derive is the pair of restrictions. On the $\rho$-real form the connection is $-\tfrac i2\cos\theta\,d\phi$, purely imaginary: $U(1)$ phase transport. On the $\sigma$-real form, $z_\theta = \tfrac\pi2+iu$, it is $-\tfrac12\sinh u\,d\phi$, purely real: $\mathbb R_+$ scale transport, with curvature $-\tfrac12\cosh u\,du\wedge d\phi$, the area form of the one-sheeted hyperboloid, unquantized **[V]**. So **phase and candidate scale transport are the two real restrictions of one holomorphic connection**, not a connection and its imaginary partner — the bridge from that transport to $\omega_{\rm obs}/\omega_{\rm em}$ being as open here as in Part XV; and near the plane, $A_\sigma(\partial_\parallel) = -y\,\partial_\perp b_\parallel|_P+O(y^3)$, odd in $y$: to leading order the scale connection on the split form is the normal derivative of the Hopf connection at the plane times the split coordinate **[D]**.

## XVII.3 Theorem C — the dual shell [T/D]

On $SL(2,\mathbb C)$ take $\rho(g) = (g^\dagger)^{-1}$, fixed set $SU(2)$, and $\sigma(g) = \bar g$, fixed set $SL(2,\mathbb R)$; their product $\jmath(g) = (g^T)^{-1}$ has fixed set $SO(2,\mathbb C)$, and on the torus $\operatorname{diag}(w,w^{-1})$ the three act as $w\mapsto\bar w^{-1}$, $\bar w$, $w^{-1}$ — the fiber actions of §XVII.1 exactly **[D]**. This is §XVI.8's Galactic involution and not merely an isomorphic pair: conjugation by $V = \tfrac1{\sqrt2}\big[\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\-i&i\end{smallmatrix}\big]$ carries the explicit lift $(z_1,z_2)\mapsto(\bar z_2,\bar z_1)$ to plain conjugation, $g\mapsto\bar g$ on $SU(2)$, rotating the Galactic axis onto the third Hopf axis **[V]**. The compact real form of the shell triple is the established $U(1)\hookrightarrow SU(2)\twoheadrightarrow S^2$; its split sibling is $\mathbb R^\times\hookrightarrow SL(2,\mathbb R)\twoheadrightarrow\{X\in\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb R):\det X = -1\}$, the one-sheeted hyperboloid — two-dimensional de Sitter space, Lorentzian where the compact base is Riemannian. The common fixed group is $SU(2)\cap SL(2,\mathbb R) = SO(2)$, whose orbit is the equator of $S^2$ and the waist of the hyperboloid — the plane's trace on the linking sphere — and $SO(2)$ double-covers that orbit with fiber $U(1)\cap\mathbb R^\times = \{\pm1\}$. The phase/scale pairing thus has a group-theoretic origin, not only a connection-level one, and §XVII.2's split curvature is this shell's area form.

## XVII.4 Theorem D — the common locus and the residual sign [D/V]

On the complexified base, with $z_\perp = n+iy$, $\rho$ fixes $y = 0$ and $\sigma$ fixes $n = 0$, so $E_\rho\cap E_\sigma$ is the Galactic plane; upstairs the total common fixed locus is the plane with fiber $\{\pm1\}$, while the $\rho$-real physical fiber over the plane remains $U(1)$. **The Galactic plane is where two inequivalent notions of reality agree**, and on it continuous phase and continuous scale both collapse to a $\mathbb Z_2$ remnant. For the bi-real equivariant Hopf connection that remnant is not merely related to the $-1$ planar holonomy of §XVI.8: it is the same fact. Parallel transport once around the equator carries the real point $+1$ to $-1$ precisely because the common fixed set $SO(2)$ is connected and double-covers the equator — the planar holonomy is the monodromy of the $\{\pm1\}$ cover, seen from the connection instead of the group **[D, for the equivariant connection]**. Topology alone gives the double cover; it does not assign an arbitrary connection's transport.

## XVII.5 The metric test, and what becomes physical [D/S]

$\rho$-reality of the complexified metric is automatic for the analytic continuation of a real metric; $\sigma$-reality requires the real metric to be $\jmath$-invariant, reflection-symmetric about the plane, which the Galaxy's geometry is to good approximation. Given that, the full canonical metric restricts to $E_\sigma$ nondegenerately with two sign flips: the normal component, since $n\to iy$; and the fiber component, since $w$ real means $\chi = i\mu$ while the continued $\mathcal A_H$ is imaginary there, so $R_H^2(d\chi+\mathcal A_H)^2\to-R_H^2(d\mu-A_\sigma)^2$. Counting all six real directions, **the split real form has split signature $(3,3)$**: time, the Galactic normal and the fiber timelike, the three in-plane directions spacelike **[D]**. The test passes with the signature characterized rather than hidden. Whether that characterization pays the branch's conceptual debt is a further question: a $(3,3)$ geometry has severe Cauchy and positivity difficulties, so signature supplies a *candidate* representation-selection reason for matter to prefer $E_\rho$ — but "localization requires exactly one time" is not a theorem derived here, and the claim is **[S/O]**, with the observer's base $B_{\rm rep}$ as a rendering (§XVI) and dynamical breaking as the alternatives.

## XVII.6 Theorem E — the dual histories are real from the other side [V/D]

For a planar seed, $z_\parallel$ real and $z_\perp = iy(s)$ along the whole history, the conjugate lifts $\gamma\pm iY_{\rm gal}$ are $\sigma$-fixed pointwise: both lie in $E_\sigma$, exchanged by $\rho$ and by $\jmath$ **[V]**. So for planar seeds the interpretation holds exactly and globally along the history: **what is imaginary from one real form is real from the other.** The Jacobi gate becomes a transition from the common locus $E_\rho\cap E_\sigma$ into the split form $E_\sigma$, where the "imaginary displacement" is a real displacement $y(s) = y_0\sin(s/\ell)$ in the timelike normal direction, obeying the same equation because the Jacobi equation is linear over $\mathbb C$. Off the plane it fails: a seed with $n\neq0$ is sent by $\sigma$ to $-n+iy$, not itself; off-plane classes are $\sigma$-exchanged mirror pairs, not $\sigma$-real. This matches §XVI.8's extension result — the normal mode extends across the round arena, its $\sigma$-reality does not — and it is the first place the two real forms are seen to do different work for planar and off-plane histories.

## XVII.7 Theorem F and the reflection theorem: the sector the parities select [V/D]

**No overconstraint.** Bi-reality does not force $\eta = 0$. On the monopole representative, $F_H(N_{\rm gal},k) = x\cdot(N\times k)/2r^3$ is nonzero on the plane, and the normal sector's scale character is nonzero and even under $\jmath$ — computed: $-3.03$ for a class and $-3.03$ for its plane-mirror **[V]**; the meridian component $F_H(\partial_{\rm mer},k)$ is $\jmath$-odd and vanishes on the plane, the meridian sector's death once more. Theorem F passes for the normal sector.

**The reflection theorem [D/V].** Let $r$ be any reflection of the base under which the Hopf data are equivariant — hence odd, by the fiber-inverting lift that §XVI.8 showed every orientation-reversing reflection of the linking sphere forces. Then, by the loop-orientation argument of §XVI.8,

$$\eta_{r\Xi} = -\eta_\Xi\ \text{ if } r_*Y = +Y,\qquad \eta_{r\Xi} = +\eta_\Xi\ \text{ if } r_*Y = -Y$$

— verified on the monopole model in both cases, an in-plane displacement changing sign under the plane reflection, $+1.21\to-1.21$, and the normal displacement changing sign under the in-plane reflection $\kappa$, $-3.03\to+3.03$ **[V]**. Four consequences follow. A sector passes the parity tests exactly when its displacement is *odd under every reflection the data respect*. The meridian sector, $Y\parallel\partial_{\rm mer}$, is even under every reflection of $B_{\rm rep}$, so it dies as soon as the data respect any. The normal sector, $Y\parallel N_{\rm gal}$, is odd under $\jmath$ and even under every in-plane reflection $\kappa$: it survives if and only if the Hopf data *break every in-plane reflection while keeping the plane reflection*. That is the symmetry class of a rotating disk — with a distinction that must be kept: the displacement $Y$ is a *polar* vector, $\jmath_*Y = -Y$ and $\kappa_*Y = +Y$, while the disk's angular momentum $L\parallel N_{\rm gal}$ is *axial*, with the opposite parities, $\jmath_*L = +L$ and $\kappa_*L = -L$. They are different representations, and $Y$ does not transform as angular momentum. What the calculation finds is this: **the surviving displacement is the polar Galactic normal, and to escape the in-plane obstruction the Hopf data must carry an axial order parameter along that normal — preserving the plane reflection, breaking the vertical ones — which a rotating disk supplies through its angular momentum.** The rotation sense can then orient the otherwise sign-ambiguous normal line, using the ambient orientation to pair the axial $L$ with one choice of $N_{\rm gal}$, and the sign of the systemic $\eta$ — red or blue — is the correlation between the twin parity of item 10 in §XI.2 and that rotation sense, a datum with a natural candidate: twin parity aligned with angular momentum. Positivity and magnitude remain the field equation's. And whether the Hopf data carry the rotation sense at all is the Kim-equivariance and spin–phase-locking question of item 8 in §XI.2 — no longer a consistency check but load-bearing.

## XVII.8 Equal footing is not equivalence; the sheets [D/S]

$U(1)$ and $\mathbb R^\times$ are inequivalent real Lie groups, and $\jmath$ is not an exchange between the real forms — it maps each to itself. No holomorphic automorphism exchanging $\rho$ and $\sigma$ is assumed, and its absence would not kill the branch; §XVII.5's signature asymmetry is the physical form of the inequivalence, and a candidate reason for one form to host matter and the other the noncompact transport. For the visual sheets of Part XVI the bi-real geometry offers cleaner definitions to test, not to assign: a self-dual sheet is $\jmath$-invariant; a planar seed lies in $E_\rho\cap E_\sigma$; its dual-history continuation lies in $E_\sigma$; its localized matter realization lies in $E_\rho$. The moduli space $\mathfrak S_O$, the population Hubble target $\eta\simeq f(D_{\rm app})$ with $f'(0) = H_0/c$, and the no-circularity rule are unchanged; bi-reality may constrain which sheets are admissible and must not supply a knob.

## XVII.9 The opening direction, and a family of second real structures [T/V/D/S/O]

*The author's next intuition: where the two paths are nearly aligned, the direction between them should be able to swing far more sharply than the direction between far-separated paths — so that the same common-real intersection that selects the Galactic plane at full scale might, in the path-aligned regime, select the equatorial plane of each Kerr black hole. (The intersection is of two real forms, not a differential 2-form.) The intuition has a precise core, but the core is about nullity and conditioning rather than smallness, and stating it precisely also supplies the decision §XVII.11's most serious kill criterion demands.*

**The core, stated precisely [T/V/S].** In the history space, $Y = 0$ has no direction: a coincident pair records nothing about how it would open. A standard geometric resolution is to blow up the zero section — the diagonal of the Jacobi bundle — so that the exceptional divisor records the projective direction $[Y]\in\mathbb P(K_\Xi)$, where $K_\Xi = \ker\mathcal J_\Xi$ is the history's admissible Jacobi kernel **[T]**; treating that exceptional divisor as physical hidden history data is the proposal **[S]**. Write $Y = \epsilon N$. A transverse perturbation turns the normalized direction by $\delta N\sim P_{N^\perp}\delta Y/\epsilon$, so near coincidence the direction is *ill-conditioned*: a perturbation of $10^{-3}$ turns it by $0.6°$ at $\epsilon = 0.1$ and by $45°$ at $\epsilon = 10^{-3}$ **[V]**. But sensitivity is not freedom. The admissible directions are $\mathbb P(K_\Xi)$, and its dimension is the nullity of the Jacobi problem: if $\dim K_\Xi = 1$ there is one direction and no orientational freedom however small $\epsilon$; if $\dim K_\Xi = 2$ there is a circle of them. Three regimes, computed **[V]**. In the round arena every transverse direction is conjugate at the antipode, $D(\pi\ell) = 0$, the kernel is the whole two-dimensional screen and $\mathbb P(K) = \mathbb{RP}^1$ with no gap. In the lumpy arena, at the old twin location $L = \pi\ell$, the map is *not* singular: both singular values are nonzero, split by factors $1.6$ to $2.3$ for classes from the Sun through the Galaxy — anisotropic near-focusing, with $\ker D(\pi\ell) = 0$, no kernel at all. The kernel reappears only at the *shifted* conjugate points: the round double focus splits into two simple conjugate points $L_{c,1}\neq L_{c,2}$, some $10^{-7}\ell$ apart, at each of which $\det D = 0$ with one singular value vanishing and the other of order $10^{-7}\ell$, so that $\dim K = 1$ with the kernel along one screen direction — astigmatic focusing. The perturbation does two separate things: it shifts the conjugate location, and it breaks the round two-dimensional degeneracy into isolated one-dimensional kernel directions. So: **near coincidence amplifies directional sensitivity; the actual freedom is the nullity at the true conjugate points, and the gap of the Jacobi spectrum decides how rigidly a direction is held.** Two sensitivities must not be merged: a small separation $\epsilon$ makes the *normalization* $Y/|Y|$ numerically sensitive, while a small Jacobi or Hessian gap makes the *selected physical direction* dynamically sensitive. For the latter the standard statement is a Davis–Kahan or Wedin bound — a selected singular direction rotates by at most of order $\|\delta D\|/\text{gap}$, the gap the distance between the relevant singular values — and the quantity $1/(1-\sigma_{\min}/\sigma_{\max})$ quoted before is a dimensionless heuristic gap indicator, not a derived susceptibility **[T]**. That is the author's intuition in falsifiable form, and it is a kill test.

**Two spaces, and the map between them [D].** A history's opening direction and the axis that selects a real form are not the same object. For a ray in the three-dimensional spatial geometry the screen is two-dimensional, so $[Y]_\Xi\in\mathbb P(K_\Xi)\subseteq\mathbb{RP}^1$; the unoriented normals of reflection planes form $\mathbb{RP}^2$. One ray's opening direction cannot parameterize every plane, and a spin axis need not lie in a given ray's screen. Version 21 already contains the cure: for the Galactic normal it did not use $N_{\rm gal}$ as the Jacobi field but its projection onto each history's transverse space, $A = e_4-p_4p-d_4d$. The general form is a map from axes to history openings,

$$\mathfrak p_\Xi:[N]\longmapsto[P^\perp_\Xi N]$$

followed by the Jacobi evolution — $Y_{\Xi,J} = P^\perp_\Xi J$ for Kerr, $Y_{\Xi,\rm gal} = P^\perp_\Xi N_{\rm gal}$ for the Galaxy — so that one projective axis produces different transverse openings on different histories, and the exceptional histories, $P^\perp_\Xi N = 0$, are predicted exactly as the pole-going great circles were. The compatibility condition is then precise: a history carries the $\sigma_{[N]}$-real opening only if $\mathfrak p_\Xi([N])\in\mathbb P(K_\Xi)$ — automatic in the round arena, where the kernel is the whole screen, and a genuine selection in the lumpy one, where a member's kernel direction must match the projected axis. That selection is how the history geometry could choose $[N]$ rather than have it assigned.

**The family, derived [T/V].** The family of second real structures is a conjugacy orbit, not a list. With $\sigma_0 = \widetilde\jmath_0\circ\rho$ for one axis, $\sigma_{[N]} = R\sigma_0R^{-1}$ for $R\in SO(3)$ carrying the axis; the stabilizer of an unoriented line is $O(2)$, so

$$\mathcal R_{\rm split}\simeq SO(3)/O(2)\simeq\mathbb{RP}^2$$

and the lifted reflections form one $SU(2)$-conjugacy orbit, $\widetilde\jmath_{[N]} = \widetilde R\,\widetilde\jmath_0\,\widetilde R^{-1}$, with the fiber inversion conjugation-invariant — checked for three hundred random elements of $SU(2)$ to $10^{-15}$ **[V]**. Smoothness and isotropy follow at once, and the family is generated by the isotropic shell symmetry rather than added. One rule: *a selected axis determines a second real form; the intersection of the two real forms determines the equilibrium plane.* No plane is pre-installed; the Galactic plane and every Kerr equatorial plane are solution-selected common-real loci. Only the unoriented line enters a reflection, which is why the projectivization is exact and why the polar/axial distinction of §XVII.7 sits comfortably: $[N]$ selects the plane, and an axial datum orients it.

**Rigidity and flexibility as conditioning [D/S].** The two-scale intuition is not about physical scale but about the spectrum of the selection problem. At the global sheet level an isolated strong minimum of the selection functional holds $[N] = [N_{\rm gal}]$ with a large orientation-selection gap; near a degenerate or nearly coincident local history the relevant Jacobi or Hessian operator has a small gap, and the selected direction responds strongly to local data. Global rigidity is a large gap, local flexibility a small one, and the gap is a calculable number — the split of the conjugate points above is its first instance. The Kerr near zone, computed in §XVII.10, turns out to be a *large*-gap regime: the mass shear holds the kernel to within about $20°$ even at the photon region's edge, so the flexibility this paragraph anticipates lives where the Jacobi spectrum is degenerate — the round arena — and not near a spinning source.

**The Kerr bridge, with its firewall [D/O].** The theory's Kerr branch already displaces the source by $z\to z-ia$ along the spin axis, with $J = Ma$, and keeps that displacement distinct from the Hopf fiber. Under $\sigma_J$ the displaced source is fixed: $\rho$ moves $z_\perp = -ia$ to $+ia$, while $\sigma_J$ leaves it where it is **[V]**. **The Newman–Janis displacement is a displacement into the split real form of $\sigma_J$** — imaginary from $\rho$, real from $\sigma_J$, the Kerr instance of Theorem E — and the ring at $z = 0$ lies on $P_J = \operatorname{Fix}\rho\cap\operatorname{Fix}\sigma_J$, the equatorial plane. The same operation recognizes both planes once their selected axis is given, with $[N] = [J]$ here; the axis is not duplicated mathematically, but it is still an input to the compatibility check. The firewall was exact at 22.4 — Kerr compatibility derived locally, Kerr selection from the history geometry open — and §XVII.10 moves it: selection is now derived at the level of symmetry and confirmed in the weak field, with the strong-field near zone open. The decisive bridge is $\text{history data}\to[N]_{\rm selected}\overset{?}{=}[J]$, and the weak field says where to look. The tidal tensor of a spinning mass has a Newtonian part $\sim GM/r^3$ whose axes are set by the ray–mass plane, and a gravitomagnetic part $\sim GJ/c^2r^4\sim(a/r)$ times that, so the spin's perturbation of the Jacobi operator is $\sim a/r$ relative to the mass's — a part in a thousand at a thousand gravitational radii, order one only in the near zone $r\sim a$ **[D]**. How far that perturbation rotates the selected direction is again a gap question, schematically $\delta[N]\sim(a/r)\times(\text{spin coupling})/\Delta_{\rm orient}$: small where the mass-only operator is well separated, potentially large near degeneracy — which is the path-aligned flexibility of the author's intuition as a perturbation-theory statement. Far from a source the mass shear selects the ray–mass plane; only near-zone or near-degenerate histories can be captured by the spin. If $[N]\to[J]$ comes out of the near-zone Jacobi geometry rather than being inserted, this section becomes important; if not, the bridge is compatibility only.

**The sheet index [S].** Part XVI's hidden fiber over a sky point may contain $[Y]$: two nearly identical paths landing at almost the same visible point with different opening directions continue into different sheets — the author's dual-path indexing scheme made literal, and most descriptive where the Jacobi gap is smallest.

**Failure tests [O].** The blow-up must arise from the Jacobi and history space, not as an added sphere of labels. The map $\mathfrak p_\Xi$ must be the correct history-space map, and the compatibility $\mathfrak p_\Xi([N])\in\mathbb P(K_\Xi)$ must be what selects members in the lumpy arena. The Kerr limit must select the axis of $z\to z-ia$ from the near-zone Jacobi geometry. The Galactic limit must collapse the freedom to the observed normal, or the mechanism predicts arbitrary large-scale planes. And above all, $[N]$ must be selected by the Jacobi kernel, source spin, monodromy or sheet Hessian, never assigned per object — otherwise this is an orientation knob, and the branch retires.

## XVII.10 The central derivation, attempted: solving the compatibility relation [T/V/D/O]

*The author asked whether the decisive calculation of §XVII.9 could be done, and how possible it is. A real part of it can be done now — exactly at the level of symmetry, numerically for a flattened Galaxy in the weak field, and for Kerr in the weak field — and what cannot yet be done is named. The relation to solve is $\mathfrak p_\Xi([N])\in\mathbb P(K_\Xi)$: an axis is compatible with a history if its projection onto that history's screen lies along the history's Jacobi kernel, so each history confines the axis to the plane spanned by its ray direction and its kernel direction, $N\in{\rm span}(k_\Xi,v_\Xi)$, and the question is whether these planes, over a family of histories, meet in one axis.*

**The symmetry theorem [D].** Let the source have a *selected* reflection plane $P$ — a spherical source is symmetric under reflection through every plane and selects none, which is why reflection symmetry alone cannot pick an axis — and take a sufficiently rich family of nonparallel histories in $P$ whose even and odd parity sectors both possess simple conjugate points. For a history lying in $P$ the screen has the basis (in-plane transverse, normal), and the cross term of the screen tidal matrix vanishes by symmetry — the potential is even in the normal coordinate, so its mixed second derivative is zero on the plane. The Jacobi system decouples, the two simple conjugate points have kernels along the in-plane transverse direction and along the normal, and the compatibility planes are ${\rm span}(k,\hat t) =$ the symmetry plane itself and ${\rm span}(k,\hat N)$ = the vertical plane containing the ray. Over all planar histories the normal branch intersects in the single axis $[N_{\rm sym}]$, and the in-plane branch intersects in the whole symmetry plane. **The relation has exactly one isolated solution, the normal $[N_P]$, and one degenerate branch, the projective line $\mathbb P(P)\simeq\mathbb{RP}^1$ of in-plane axes.** The theorem needs the selected plane, the rich planar family and the simple conjugate modes, and no more. The Galaxy supplies them in the tested model; Kerr supplies the reflection plane, while the existence of the required odd conjugate branch on equatorial photon histories is an explicit condition and an open strong-field test.

**The Galaxy, numerically [V].** On a Miyamoto–Nagai disk of $6\times10^{10}M_\odot$ with scale lengths $3$ and $0.3$ kpc, histories were launched from sources inside the disk in random directions, the two-dimensional screen Jacobi system with its cross terms integrated through the Galaxy and continued analytically in the round arena, the two shifted conjugate points found as the zeros of $\det D(L)$, and each kernel direction assigned to a branch by its alignment with the projected normal. The constraint tensor $\sum m m^T$, $m = k\times v$, has a lone small eigenvalue when the planes meet in one axis and two small ones when they are degenerate. In the flattened model studied here the normal branch focuses first — a property of the model, not of the theorem. Planar histories: eigenvalues $(0,0.39,0.61)$, common axis $0.0°$ from the disk normal, the other branch $(0,0,1)$ — the theorem to machine precision. Thin-disk histories, $|z|<0.1$ kpc and inclinations under $10°$: eigenvalues $(0.041,0.41,0.55)$, common axis $4.3°$ from the normal, per-history misalignment of the kernel from the projected normal $10°$ at the median and $20°$ at the ninetieth percentile. Mildly inclined, under $20°$: $13°$ with eigenvalue $0.115$; steeply inclined, under $70°$: $3°$ with eigenvalue $0.072$ and median scatter $15°$. The conjugate-point split is $2.8\times10^{-6}\ell$ on the plane and $3$–$6\times10^{-7}\ell$ off it. **Over histories through the smooth Galaxy the compatibility relation has an isolated solution: exact on the plane, and recovered to $3°$–$13°$ in the sampled off-plane families, with per-history scatter of $10°$ at the median and $20°$ at the ninetieth percentile.** $[N]\to[N_{\rm gal}]$ is recovered from the Jacobi kernels by an axis-recovery algorithm to which the disk plane is not supplied — the source contains its plane, and the solver finds it blind.

**The obstruction stars raise, and how the sheet clears it [D/V, conditional].** The smooth disk's astigmatic split is $5\times10^{-7}$, and a star matches it at an impact parameter of $0.06$ pc; along one arena-length history about thirty encounters are closer than that, and the closest is typically at $0.01$ pc, contributing a shear thirty times the disk's at a random orientation. **A single history's kernel direction is set by its nearest star, not by the disk: per-history selection of $N_{\rm gal}$ fails.** The rescue at the sheet level is not a $\sqrt N$ assumption. The shear magnitude has a Pareto-index-one, Cauchy-like survival tail — $P(\gamma>g)\propto1/g$ from the nearest-neighbour statistics — so its variance diverges and an average of shears would fail — but the constraint tensor weights every history's *direction* equally, and a history's bias toward the disk direction is bounded, with finite mean $\langle b_{\min}^2\rangle/b_{\rm eq}^2 = 1/(\pi nLb_{\rm eq}^2)\approx1/29$. A Monte Carlo over $2\times10^5$ histories, each with its forty nearest encounters at random orientations plus the coherent disk term, gives a mean directional bias of $0.018$ with scatter $0.71$, so the sheet's constraint tensor recovers the disk direction with signal-to-noise $0.025\sqrt N$: unity at $N\approx1600$ independent histories, a margin of eleven at $2\times10^5$ **[V]**. The scaling law is derived; two things remain conditional. The sheet's selection functional is assumed to average the relation over its members in the least-squares sense, and the number of *independent* stellar configurations a sheet samples is an estimate — histories closer in angle than about $b_{\min}/\ell\approx6\times10^{-7}$ rad share their nearest star, so an arcminute island samples some $2\times10^5$ — with the real test being the covariance statistics of the sheet's constraint tensor under a realistic stellar population, including the bulge, where the density is ten times higher and the bias ten times smaller. Within those conditions $N_{\rm gal}$ is selected by the sheet and by no member of it — the concrete form of §XVII.9's global rigidity and of §XVI.6's coherence condition. The canonical normal is then an *order parameter of the collective sheet*, $[N]_\alpha = \arg\min_{[N]}\sum_{\Xi\in\mathscr L_\alpha}d^2\big(\mathfrak p_\Xi([N]),\mathbb P(K_\Xi)\big)$, sharply defined while each member's opening direction wanders — the cleanest form yet of the author's intuition that zoomed-in dual paths may be highly variable while the full galaxy has a rigid canonical normal.

**Kerr [D/O].** The theorem applies with Kerr's equatorial plane as the selected plane, provided the odd conjugate branch exists on equatorial photon histories: the compatibility relation then has the isolated solution $[J]$ and the degenerate $\mathbb{RP}^1$ branch. Its significance is stated carefully. The Kerr metric already contains its axis, so this is not the prediction of an unknown direction; it shows that the axis-selection rule is compatible with, and reconstructs blind, the intrinsic symmetry axis the geometry encodes — the selection §XVII.9 left open, at the level of symmetry. The genuinely new test is the oblique history, where the kernel is no longer symmetry-normal. In the weak field, with the spin's effective potential $(G/c)(J\times x)\cdot\hat k/r^3$, the principal kernel direction of an oblique history rotates by about $40°\times(a/b)$ *in the tested weak-field family* — $2°$ at $a/b = 0.05$, $8°$ at $0.2$ — and by exactly zero for equatorial histories and for the impact direction perpendicular to the plane of the ray and the axis **[V]**. The coefficient depends on trajectory, impact orientation, integration limits and the unperturbed gap; the general law remains $\delta[N]\sim(a/b)\times\text{coupling}/\Delta_{\rm orient}$, and the symmetry-protected zeros are more robust than the number. The strong-field near zone was the open Kerr calculation; it is now computed.

**The strong field, computed [V].** Null geodesics of Kerr were integrated from their Hamiltonian in Boyer–Lindquist coordinates, the screen Jacobi map obtained by linearizing the geodesic flow — central differences of neighbouring rays, $10^{-5}$ rad apart, projected on the screen — and conjugate points found as the zeros of the screen determinant; on Schwarzschild the machinery returns one conjugate point per ray with its kernel exactly along the tangential direction, $0.00°$, at the strong-deflection focal distance. Then the compatibility relation was solved *blind* over isotropic families of histories — random directions, random impact directions, impact parameters $6$–$12M$ in the moderate field and $3$–$6M$ in the near zone, captured rays discarded — so that any selected axis could come only from the spin. Three results. First, the twist: the spin rotates a history's kernel away from the Schwarzschild tangential direction by a median of $2°$ in the moderate field and $7.5°$ in the near zone at $a = 0.95$, with a ninetieth percentile of $13°$ and a maximum of $15°$; for near-polar oblique rays approaching the photon region the twist rises from $5°$ at $b = 8M$ to $12°$ at $b = 4.6M$ — periapsis $2.7M$, $463°$ of azimuth swept — and saturates there, a second conjugate point appearing with a twist of $18°$; the weak-field law $40°\times a/b$ underpredicts the edge by some forty percent, and the equatorial and symmetric-impact configurations twist by nothing, as the parity argument requires. Second, the constraint tensor: with the anisotropy of the directions scaling as $\sin^2$ of the twist, about $0.02$, an isotropic family cannot select the spin axis — the selection strength is $0.09$–$0.10$ at $a = 0$, the sampling floor, and $0.27$–$0.45$ at $a = 0.95$ with the selected axis $48°$ from the spin axis, indistinguishable from noise at forty-five rays. Third, therefore: **the Kerr near zone is not a small-gap regime.** The astigmatic split there is large, the kernel is held within about $20°$ of the mass shear's direction even for winding rays, and the spin does not capture the opening direction. **Spin selects the axial geometry globally; it only twists individual dual-history directions locally.** The Kerr axis is reconstructed from the spin-selected axial symmetry by the symmetry theorem above — a planar equatorial family, as blind reconstruction — and is not recovered from the local spin-induced twisting of an otherwise isotropic history family; the large near-zone reorientation that §XVII.9 contemplated does not occur for escaping rays at $a = 0.95$. Untested: extremal spin, the exact photon-orbit limit, and rays that fall in.

**Numerical validation [V].** Because the kernel angle is now the central observable, it was checked four ways. *Convergence:* finite differences at ray separations of $10^{-4}$, $10^{-5}$ and $10^{-6}$ rad, an independent method — the tangent-linear geodesic flow, integrating the exact linearization of Hamilton's equations, which is the geodesic-deviation equation in coordinates — and a grid of $8000$ rather than $3000$ points agree to $0.04°$ on every test ray. *Screen convention:* a parallel-propagated Sachs basis is not needed for the two observables used, the conjugate points and the kernel's initial direction. A null-preserving variation has $J\cdot k = 0$ throughout, so at a conjugate point the Jacobi field is a multiple of $k$, whose spatial part lies along the coordinate velocity that the projection removes exactly; the zeros of the screen determinant, and the right singular vector at them, are therefore independent of the final screen basis. *Source distance:* the one systematic found is a finite-source-distance term exactly linear in $1/R_0$. Where the impact vector is perpendicular to the ray–axis plane the twist is $0.78°$, $0.31°$ and $0.16°$ for sources at $60$, $150$ and $300M$, extrapolating to $0.00°$ with no residual: the flat $0.8°$ of the first run was this term, and for a source at infinity the configuration is untwisted. *Invariant values:* extrapolated to a source at infinity, the near-polar ray at $b = 8M$ twists by $5.72°$, and the photon-region edge ray, matched in periapsis at $2.73M$, by $10.8°$ and $17.3°$ at its two conjugate points; the near-zone family median is $7.6°$ and $8.2°$ for sources at $60$ and $150M$ — the farther launch brings the same rays slightly deeper — so about $8°$, with the ninetieth percentile at $12$–$13°$. *The weak-field law, recognized:* the coefficient $40°\times a/b$ is $\sin45°$ radian, matching the standard weak-deflection result **[T]** that a Kerr lens acts at first order as a Schwarzschild lens displaced by $a\sin i$ perpendicular to the projected spin — which rotates the shear axes by $a\sin i/b$ and leaves the perpendicular-impact configuration untwisted, as found; the strong field raises it by about $20\%$ at $b = 8M$ and $30\%$ at the edge. The conclusions stand at the degree level: the spin twists, the twist saturates near $11°$–$17°$, and isotropic families select no axis — selection strength $0.27$–$0.34$ against a sampling floor of $0.18$, the "selected" axis $49°$–$62°$ from the spin.

**Galaxy and Kerr, contrasted.** The same compatibility machinery selects axes through different regimes of organization, not one microscopic mechanism:

| | the Galaxy | Kerr |
|:--|:--|:--|
| individual histories | noisy — each kernel set by its nearest star | twisted by the spin, by at most about $17°$ |
| averaging | the sheet's constraint tensor recovers $N_{\rm gal}$ | an isotropic family does not recover $J$ |
| where the axis comes from | a collective order parameter of the disk's sheet | the spin-selected axial symmetry, through an equatorial family |



**What is established and what is not.** Established: for a source with a selected reflection plane and the required simple conjugate branches, the compatibility relation isolates the symmetry normal while retaining an in-plane $\mathbb{RP}^1$ branch; for the smooth Galaxy that solution is exact on the plane and within $3°$–$13°$ off it, with $10°$–$20°$ per-history scatter; stellar randomization destroys per-history selection, and a derived $\sqrt N$ law with a conditional effective $N$ restores it at the sheet level; and for Kerr the axis is reconstructed by the same theorem, with the spin twisting kernels by at most about $20°$ from the weak field to the photon region's edge, so that no isotropic family selects it. Not established: the sheet's selection functional and which conjugate point renders; extremal spin and the exact photon-orbit limit; and whether the selected $[N]$ then feeds the redshift sector as §XVII.7 requires, since that sector still needs an axial datum along the selected axis. The family of §XVII.9 is no longer only an orientation catalogue: it has a selection mechanism, one theorem and one numerical confirmation deep.

**Reproducibility record.** So that the coefficients above can later be told apart from artifacts of one implementation. *Galaxy kernels:* Miyamoto–Nagai potential, $M = 6\times10^{10}M_\odot$, $a = 3$ kpc, $b = 0.3$ kpc, $\ell = 16$ kpc; sources at cylindrical radius uniform in $4$–$9$ kpc, uniform azimuth, height uniform within the stated $|z|$ bound, directions at uniform azimuth and inclination uniform within the stated bound; the screen Jacobi system $J'' = -(\ell^{-2}+T)J$ with $T = (2/c^2)E^T\nabla\nabla\Phi\,E$ in the screen basis $E$ (projected normal, its cross product with the ray), Hessian by central differences at $2$ pc, integrated to $45$ kpc at relative tolerance $10^{-10}$ with steps of at most $50$ pc, then continued analytically in the round arena, $D(L) = D(s_1)\cos((L-s_1)/\ell)+\ell D'(s_1)\sin((L-s_1)/\ell)$; conjugate points as the sign changes of $\det D(L)$ on $4001$ points within $\pm2\times10^{-5}$ of $\pi\ell$, refined by bracketing; kernel direction the last right singular vector; branch assignment by alignment with the projected normal; constraint tensor $C = \sum m m^T$ with $m = k\times v$ normalized, the common axis its smallest eigenvector; twelve to eighteen histories per family, generator seed $3$. *Stellar Monte Carlo:* $n = 0.1\,{\rm pc}^{-3}$ stars of one solar mass, path length $L = 30$ kpc inside the disk, per-star shear $2GM\ell/c^2b^2$; the squared impact parameters of each history's forty nearest encounters drawn as a Poisson process in $b^2$ of rate $\pi nL$; orientations uniform, entering the traceless screen tensor as $\gamma e^{2i\phi}$; the coherent disk term $5\times10^{-7}$ along the normal; each history's direction $\theta = \tfrac12\arg Z$ of the summed tensor $Z$, the statistic $\langle\cos2\theta\rangle$ and its scatter; $2\times10^5$ histories, seed $1$. *Kerr strong field:* $M = 1$, Boyer–Lindquist coordinates, Hamilton's equations for the null geodesic with the analytic inverse metric and its $r,\theta$ derivatives, DOP853 at relative tolerance $10^{-11}$, launch at radius $60$ from position $-60\hat k+b\hat b$ with the flat-space null momentum corrected to $H = 0$, integration stopped at radius $150$, at $r_++0.02$, or at $\sin\theta<0.02$ (rays on the axis discarded); Jacobi map from four rays perturbed by $\pm10^{-5}$ rad along the screen axes, separations at equal affine parameter projected on the local screen, conjugate points bracketed on $3000$ points and bisected; twist the angle between the kernel and $\hat k\times\hat b$; families of $20$ rays ($45$ for the near zone at $a = 0.95$), seeds $2$ and $5$; the winding sweep at inclination $45°$ with the impact vector $5°$ off the ray–axis plane. *Validation:* ray separations $10^{-4}$ to $10^{-6}$ rad; the tangent-linear flow from the symbolic Jacobian of Hamilton's equations, its initial tangents by central differences of the launch map at $10^{-7}$; launch distances $60$, $150$ and $300M$ with the exit radius at $2.5R_0$; twists extrapolated as $T_\infty+c/R_0$ by least squares; the edge ray matched in periapsis by bisection in $b$; the near-zone family of thirty escaping rays, seed $5$, at two launch distances. *Kerr weak field:* units $GM/c^2 = 1$, effective potential $-1/r+a(\hat z\times x)\cdot\hat k/r^3$ with $a = 1$, the traceless screen Hessian integrated along a straight ray over $\pm80$ at step $0.02$, the rotation of its principal eigenvector between $a = 0$ and $a = 1$, over seven impact directions at inclination $45°$.

## XVII.11 Kill criteria, recovery, verdict, program [D]

Against the brief's ten kill criteria, on the local model: commuting antiholomorphic involutions exist (1); both preserve the complex shell (2); a nondegenerate complex metric satisfies both reality conditions, the split slice ultrahyperbolic (3); one holomorphic connection satisfies both conditions and retains the normal sector's mixed curvature (4); the split principal bundle and quotient exist locally, globally open (5); the meridian datum is $\jmath$-even and so real for both structures (6); the normal dual histories are $\sigma$-real for planar seeds only (7, partial); bi-reality forces $\eta = 0$ only for the meridian sector (8); no new fields, connections or gravity (9); the matter–scale asymmetry is explained by signature rather than inserted (10, candidate). An eleventh criterion, added at 22.1 and the most serious: **no preferred-plane smuggling.** If $(E_{\mathbb C},\rho,\sigma)$ is primitive, then $\operatorname{Fix}\rho\cap\operatorname{Fix}\sigma$ already installs the Galactic plane in the fundamental data, undoing the virtue of Part XVI, which set out to derive the special normal rather than insert it; $\sigma$ must be selected by the galactic solution or by existing canonical data, never supplied as an arbitrary new axis. Two versions of the branch are legitimate — global primitive bi-reality, or a solution-dependent second real structure $\sigma_{\rm gal}$ with $(E_{\mathbb C},\rho)$ fundamental — and the part must decide rather than slide between them; §XVII.9 decides, for the family: the primitive datum is the family of admissible second real structures, and the member is solution-selected. Every version-21 structure is recovered by restriction to $E_\rho$. Charge is not identified with optical scale: $\mu_{\rm charge}\neq\eta_{\rm optical}$ until a common representation is derived.

| grade | contents |
|:--|:--|
| **standard** | real forms of complex Lie groups and their fixed loci; $SL(2,\mathbb R)$ and the one-sheeted hyperboloid; equivariance of connections under antiholomorphic involutions; signature under analytic continuation |
| **verified here** | the $\mathbb Z_2\times\mathbb Z_2$ of $\rho,\sigma,\jmath$ on the local model; the diamond $U(1)$, $\mathbb R^\times$, $\{\pm1\}$; both reality conditions and their product on the Hopf connection; the phase and scale restrictions and the hyperboloid curvature; $\sigma$-reality of planar dual histories; the reflection theorem in both cases; no overconstraint for the normal sector; the family $\sigma_{[N]}$ as one $SU(2)$-conjugacy orbit, $SO(3)/O(2) = \mathbb{RP}^2$ on the base; the ill-conditioning of the opening direction near coincidence; the Jacobi kernel two-dimensional in the round arena, absent at the lumpy arena's old twin location where the singular values split by $1.6$ to $2.3$, and one-dimensional at each of the two shifted simple conjugate points into which the round focus splits; the Kerr source fixed by $\sigma_J$; the compatibility relation solved over histories through a flattened Galaxy, exact on the plane and isolated within $3°$–$13°$ in the sampled off-plane families; the Monte Carlo of stellar randomization, directional bias $0.018$ and signal-to-noise $0.025\sqrt N$; the weak-field Kerr kernel rotation $\approx40°\times a/b$ in the tested family, zero for equatorial histories; the strong-field Kerr computation — kernels twisted by at most about $20°$ up to the photon region's edge at $a = 0.95$, isotropic families selecting no axis, the near zone a large-gap regime — validated: converged to $0.04°$ across two independent methods, with a finite-source-distance term linear in $1/R_0$ that vanishes at infinity; invariant twists $5.7°$ at $b = 8M$ and $10.8°$ and $17.3°$ at the photon-region edge; the weak-field coefficient identified as the displaced-lens rotation $a\sin i/b$ |
| **derived here** | the dual shell $SL(2,\mathbb R)$ over $dS_2$ with common group $SO(2)$; the residual sign as the planar holonomy; the split form of split signature $(3,3)$; the surviving sector as the polar Galactic normal, with the Hopf data required to carry an axial order parameter along it, supplied by the disk's rotation; the symmetry theorem — for a selected reflection plane with a rich planar family and simple conjugate modes, one isolated compatible axis and one degenerate $\mathbb{RP}^1$ branch, Kerr included; per-history selection destroyed by the nearest star, the $\sqrt N$ recovery law derived from bounded directional bias, the effective $N$ conditional |
| **neutral** | the oddness of the connection — assumed as $\sigma$-reality, exactly version 21's conditional under another name |
| **open** | global existence of $\sigma$ and of the split quotient $E_\sigma/\mathbb R^\times$; the meridian datum's signed transformation law beyond the local model; whether the Hopf data carry the disk's rotation sense (item 8); the twin parity as angular momentum (item 10); signature as the reason matter prefers the compact form; $\sigma$-reality for off-plane classes; the sheets in the bi-real geometry; the map $\mathfrak p_\Xi$ as the correct history-space map, and its compatibility with the Jacobi kernel as the selection mechanism in the lumpy arena; extremal spin and the exact photon-orbit limit; the sheet's selection functional and which conjugate point renders; and whether the selected axis feeds the redshift sector, which still needs an axial datum; the collapse of $[N]$ to the Galactic normal at full scale as a large selection gap |

**Verdict.** At the local level the branch derives more than it assumes: one assumption — that the connection respects the second real structure, which is version 21's conditional restated — buys the compact/split diamond, the two restrictions of one connection, the Lorentzian dual shell, the identity of the residual sign with the planar holonomy, the reality of planar dual histories, a candidate signature explanation of the matter–scale asymmetry, and the selection of the surviving redshift sector as the polar Galactic normal with an axial symmetry-breaking datum. §XVII.10 gives the family its selection mechanism: for a source with a selected reflection plane and the required simple conjugate branches, the compatibility relation isolates the symmetry normal, realized for the smooth Galaxy to $3°$–$13°$ off the plane, recovered at the sheet level from stellar randomization by a derived scaling law with a conditional effective $N$, and reconstructing the Kerr axis by the same theorem. The family of §XVII.9 adds the decision the no-smuggling criterion required — the split type as a conjugacy class generated by the shell symmetry — types the opening direction correctly as $\mathbb P(K_\Xi)$ with the map $\mathfrak p_\Xi$ from axes to history openings, turns the two-scale intuition into a statement about nullity and spectral gap, and gives a Kerr bridge in which the theory's own complex displacement is a displacement into the split real form, with the firewall that compatibility is derived and selection is not. It does not yet earn modification of the core: the global structure, the split quotient, the rotation-sense requirement and the selection of $[N]$ by the history geometry are open, and version 21.7 remains the control. Version 22 is frozen here as an experimental branch beside the frozen control, version 21.7: the concepts stop being versioned, and what remains is a research program of concrete calculations. **The program, in order:** whether the Hopf data carry the disk's rotation sense (item 8 of §XI.2, now load-bearing); the twin parity as angular momentum (item 10); then Part XV's mixed-curvature field equation with the normal sector as its target and the finite-width test after it; then the most consequential computation of the branch — the sheet-level constraint tensor built in a realistic lumpy Galactic model, with correlated stars, bulge, spiral structure and the actual stationary-history weights, to see whether its smallest eigendirection stays sharply on $N_{\rm gal}$ — then extremal spin at the photon-orbit limit, and the sheet's selection functional; then the split quotient and the global family; then the sheets.

# PART XVIII — CONFORMAL REPACKING OVER AN INVARIANT GRAVITY QUOTIENT: THE VERSION-23 BRANCH [T/V/D/S/O]

*The author's next set of ideas turns from selection to identity and budget: whether switching among selections and redistributing a body's charge, spin and nonextremality can both be operations of one conformal transformation on the pre-quotient space, tuning the agreeing structure that lives in the quotient; whether seemingly separate spacetime points are one underlying point with different agreeing structure, extremal Reissner–Nordström and its inversion being the test; whether extremality, with its traceless optical Ricci tensor, is an idealized case and departure from it "beefs up a connection"; whether two bodies share a budget; whether an electric field is the imprint of conditioning on the outside universe that cannot be settled; and whether a Boltzmannian counting of renderings makes the Galactic frame typical rather than fundamental. This part builds on structures the document already has — agreeing structure (§XIII.4), centrades and the Kerr–Newman budget (§XIII.5, §X.10), the Hopf-sign budget (§V.4) — and it begins where the mathematics is exact: static charged holes, before spinning ones. Parts 0–XVII are unchanged; version 21.7 remains the control and version 22.9 the experimental bi-real branch.*

## XVIII.0 The thesis, and what it rests on [S]

**The thesis.** A physical spacetime is a rendering of invariant canonical data; centrade fields measure the obstruction to unpacking that data simultaneously; and conformal repackings move between renderings while preserving the gravitational quotient. The author's constraint is the last clause: whatever a repacking does upstairs — grow fiber lengths, alter how centres work — it must preserve the gravity-base metric that controls the fiber lengths.

**What it rests on.** Three exact footholds, all verified below: the optical geometry of Reissner–Nordström, whose scalar curvature *is* the nonextremality; the Majumdar–Papapetrou family, optically scalar-flat for any number of extremal centres; and the Couch–Torrence inversion, an exact isometry of the extremal optical geometry that renders one radial class twice. These are not proposals. They are the reason this branch can start on the ground.

## XVIII.1 The budget, corrected [T/D]

The identity as first written — $a^2/M^2+Q^2/M^2+(Q^2-M^2)/M^2 = 1$ — is not the Kerr–Newman bound. The exact statement is §X.10's sphere: with $\delta^2 := M^2-a^2-Q^2$, subextremality is $\delta^2\geq0$ and

$$w_a+w_Q+w_\delta = 1,\qquad w_a = \frac{a^2}{M^2},\quad w_Q = \frac{Q^2}{M^2},\quad w_\delta = \frac{\delta^2}{M^2}$$

with all three weights in $[0,1]$: a simplex of unpacking weights, which is exactly the shape a fixed-budget redistribution needs. Two cautions keep it honest. The three are not independent — $\delta$ is fixed by the other two at given $M$ — and the sphere is an identity of general relativity, not a symmetry: charge, angular momentum and $\delta$ have different transformation properties, and nothing rotates one into another. So a black hole is **one centrade with three channels**, $C_{\rm BH}\mapsto(w_a,w_Q,w_\delta)$, rather than three centrades; and two black holes are two centrades whose admissible unpackings need not factor, $\mathscr U_{\rm adm}\neq P_1\times P_2$ — the non-product structure §XIII.5 already defined, and the place where the author's two-body budget belongs.

## XVIII.2 The optical geometry of Reissner–Nordström: nonextremality is the trace [V]

For $ds^2 = -f\,dt^2+dr^2/f+r^2d\Omega^2$ with $f = 1-2M/r+Q^2/r^2$, the optical metric is $h_{\rm opt} = dr^2/f^2+(r^2/f)\,d\Omega^2$, and a direct computation of its scalar curvature gives

$$R[h_{\rm opt}] = -\frac{6\,(M^2-Q^2)}{r^4},\qquad\text{so}\qquad \delta^2 = -\frac{r^4}{6}R_{\rm opt}$$

**[V]**. **Extremality is optical scalar-flatness throughout the exterior** — scalar-flat, not Ricci-flat: at $Q = M$ the full optical Ricci tensor survives, $|{\rm Ric}_{\rm opt}|^2 = 24M^2(r-M)^6/r^{12}$ **[V]**. In three dimensions the Riemann tensor is the Ricci tensor, so the decomposition ${\rm Ric} = \tfrac13R\,h+\mathring{\rm Ric}$ with $|{\rm Ric}|^2 = R^2/3+|\mathring{\rm Ric}|^2$ **[V]** is a complete accounting, and it separates what is exact from what is proposed. Exact: $R_{\rm opt}\propto-\delta^2$, and at extremality all remaining optical curvature is traceless. Proposed **[S]**: the branch's dictionary, that the trace is the unresolved scalar budget and the traceless part is relational centrade structure. The decomposition is standard; the reading is a structural proposal and is graded as one.

**The author's "neat Laplacian", exact [V].** A spherically symmetric three-metric is conformally flat, and in the isotropic radius $\sigma$, with $r = \sigma+M+\delta^2/4\sigma$, the optical metric is $h_{\rm opt} = W^4(d\sigma^2+\sigma^2d\Omega^2)$ with $W^4 = r^2/\sigma^2f$. Its conformal factor obeys

$$\Delta W = \frac34\,\frac{\delta^2}{r^4}\,W^5,\qquad\text{equivalently}\qquad R_{\rm opt} = -8W^{-5}\Delta W$$

checked at four charges and three radii to five figures **[V]**: a Poisson equation whose source is the nonextremality, so that $W$ is harmonic exactly at extremality — $W = \sqrt{1+M/\sigma}$ — and the superposition principle for optical conformal factors is lost the moment $\delta\neq0$. That is the precise form of "losing the neat Laplacian condition": nonextremality is the source term, and extremality is the sector in which optical conformal factors add. And the conformal factor has a name in this theory. With the lapse $N$ and the spatial conformal factor $\Omega$ of the isotropic form, $W^4 = \Omega^2/N^2$ exactly, so $W = \sqrt{\Omega/N}$ **[V]**: the trace of the optical Ricci tensor is the Laplacian of the square root of the ratio between the spatial conformal factor — which locks the Hopf fiber length, $L_H = \Omega L_H^{\rm ref}$ — and the inverse lapse. §X.2 found that $N\Omega = 1$ holds precisely for extremal holes; here that is the statement that at extremality the locked fiber length is the inverse lapse, $W = \Omega = U$ is harmonic, and the optical trace vanishes. The exact statements are these: $N\Omega = 1$ at extremality, and $\delta^2$ sources $\Delta\sqrt{\Omega/N}$ away from it. Calling the latter *the mismatch between the fiber-length locking and the lapse* is the theory's interpretation of it **[S]** — the author's "beefing up" in the architecture's own terms, a mismatch of two scalars it already has, not yet a connection's curvature.

## XVIII.3 Many extremal centres [V]

Majumdar–Papapetrou, $ds^2 = -U^{-2}dt^2+U^2d\mathbf x^2$ with $U = 1+\sum_iM_i/|\mathbf x-\mathbf x_i|$ harmonic off the centres, has optical metric $h_{\rm opt} = U^4\,d\mathbf x^2$, and for a three-dimensional conformal metric $R[U^4\delta] = -8U^{-5}\Delta U$ **[V]**. So **every multi-centre extremal configuration is optically scalar-flat off its centres**: the balance of gravitational attraction and like-charge repulsion, the harmonicity of $U$, and the vanishing of the optical scalar curvature are one condition. Two equal centres at separation $2M$, computed: $R_{\rm opt}$ vanishes to $10^{-17}$ along the axis; $|{\rm Ric}_{\rm opt}|^2$ vanishes deep in each throat and at infinity alike — the deep throat is Bertotti–Robinson, whose optical geometry is flat, so the configuration has *three optically flat ends* — peaks near each photon sphere, and at the midpoint carries a value, $1.6\times10^{-3}M^{-4}$, unavailable to any single extremal hole of the same total mass $2M$, whose maximum in the same units is $24/(2^{12}\cdot16) = 3.7\times10^{-4}$: relational curvature of the pair **[V]**. Part III's frozen-matter constraint — extremal holes in static balance — is the same sector seen from Law I.

**The two-centrade test [V/T].** Does a non-product constraint arise intrinsically when two centres coexist? Within the extremal static sector, no: Majumdar–Papapetrou is linear, any masses at any positions, an exact product $P_1\times P_2$ of the two extremal states. The constraint is the requirement of extremality itself. At leading order two charged bodies feel $(Q_1Q_2-M_1M_2)/d^2$, so static balance needs $Q_1Q_2 = M_1M_2$ — in simplex weights $w_{Q,1}w_{Q,2} = 1$ with each weight in $[0,1]$, hence both equal to one **[V]**: inside the physical, subextremal sector no static two-body configuration exists at all, and a pair that leaves the extremal slice cannot stay static — it must move, radiate, or hold a strut, which in the exact static two-body solutions appears as a conical singularity unless a balance condition on masses, charges and separation is met **[T]**. Beyond leading order the statement is exact: the static electrovacuum spacetimes containing more than one black hole and an asymptotically flat slice with compact interior are classified, and they are Majumdar–Papapetrou (Chruściel–Tod) **[T]** — two subextremal charged black holes admit no static configuration free of struts at any order. The result is best recorded as a negative one. With the charge signs $s_i = \operatorname{sgn}Q_i$ restored — the weights $w_Q$ discard them — the regular static two-hole set is $(E_+\times E_+)\cup(E_-\times E_-)$: both extremal, with like signs. That is a discrete same-sign correlation and, within each component, an exact product; there is no continuous law by which lowering one hole's charge is compensated by a change on the other so that equilibrium survives. Leave extremality on either body and the pair leaves the regular static sector. **The static two-centrade compensation hypothesis fails: static coexistence is a sector constraint, not a redistribution law.** The sector boundary is exactly $R_{\rm opt} = 0$ of §XVIII.2, and genuine inter-body compensation, if it exists, must be sought in the dynamical, history or repacking sector.

## XVIII.4 One point, two appearances: the inversion and the identifier [T/V/D]

Extremal Reissner–Nordström carries the Couch–Torrence inversion: with $\rho = r-M$, $\rho\mapsto M^2/\rho$ is a conformal isometry of the spacetime **[T]** and an *exact isometry of the optical geometry* — $h_{\rm opt} = (1+M/\rho)^4(d\rho^2+\rho^2d\Omega^2)$ pulls back to itself — fixing the photon sphere $\rho = M$, $r = 2M$ **[V]**. So the toy model of the author's "one underlying point, two appearances" is exact and static: the underlying class is the inversion orbit $[\rho] = \{\rho,M^2/\rho\}$, the near-horizon and asymptotic regions are its two appearances, and the photon sphere is the fixed locus. The author's guess about the identifier is also right: in $x = (r-M)/M$,

$$M^4|{\rm Ric}_{\rm opt}|^2 = \frac{24x^6}{(1+x)^{12} }$$

is exactly invariant under $x\to1/x$, stationary at $x = 1$, and monotone on each side of the photon sphere **[V]** — with the angles, a two-to-one label of inversion pairs. **The squared magnitude of the optical Ricci tensor tracks the identification.** What the calculation does not do is prove the two appearances are one point; it makes the hypothesis calibrated, with the fixed sphere as its test bed. And the two-centre case sharpens it: with three optically flat ends the identifier becomes many-to-one — throat one, throat two and infinity all read "flat" — which is exactly why identity must carry transport (§XVIII.5).

## XVIII.5 Identity with transport: the rendering groupoid [S]

An equivalence relation cannot depend on the path: $x\sim y$, full stop. The author's requirement — that whether one is on the "up" or "down" version of a point is not unique to the point but emergent from where one moved from and to — is a groupoid with a connection. Objects are rendered appearances; morphisms are admissible history transports $T_\gamma:\mathcal H_x\to\mathcal H_y$; two paths give $T_\gamma\neq T_{\gamma'}$, and the loop $\gamma'^{-1}\gamma$ has a holonomy. Same underlying point is then relational identity equipped with transport, and nothing new is needed to build it: the theory already has connections, history classes, holonomies and projective opening directions (Parts XV–XVII), and this branch upgrades the history connection to the identity transport of the rendering groupoid. The extremal miniature is the inversion orbit with a transition element $g_{+-}$ between its two appearances — $\mathbb Z_2$, or a Hopf sign $-1$ — to be *derived*, not assumed: Couch–Torrence gives the double appearance and not the sign. The structure the theory already has with such a sign is Structure III: the two crossings of one meridian carry opposite Chern signs, so a source and its twin are two throats with the twin parity between them; whether an extremal pair is a twin pair is a question for the arena, not the local model.

**The transition sign, decided — and a retraction [V/D].** The sign need not be assumed, but the right invariant is the degree of the identification on the linking sphere, not the ambient orientation, and version 23.1 used the wrong one. The midplane reflection that exchanges two equal Majumdar–Papapetrou throats restricts to the linking sphere with degree $-1$, and so does the antipodal map that carries a meridian crossing to its twin; each reverses the Chern sign and, by the lemma of §XVI.8, lifts to the Hopf bundle only with fiber inversion **[V]**. The Couch–Torrence inversion does not: written in Cartesian form, $x\mapsto M^2x/|x|^2$ carries $r\hat n$ to $(M^2/r)\hat n$, so its induced map between linking spheres is $\hat n\mapsto\hat n$, of degree $+1$ — the ambient Jacobian is negative because the radial direction reverses, but the angular sphere's orientation is preserved, and $I^*c_1 = +c_1$ **[V]**. So the Chern class forces no fiber inversion for the inversion pair, and version 23.1's claim that the twin parity, the Couch–Torrence pair and the throat exchange are one phenomenon is **retracted** for the second of the three: the twin parity and the throat exchange are; the inversion's transition element, if it carries anything, must come from the history connection of Parts XV–XVII and is open. What survives is the groupoid structure itself: identification by a degree $-1$ symmetry inverts the fiber, transport along a path does not, their composition is a loop with antilinear holonomy, and whether the far spot reads as the "up" or the "down" version of the near one depends on which morphism carried the comparison — the author's relational identity, for the pairs that have it.

## XVIII.6 Repacking transformations, and the fork [S/D]

A pure conformal rescaling, $\hat g\to\Omega^2\hat g$ with the same readout maps, changes no agreeing structure: the kernels $K_i$ of §XIII.4 are unchanged, so $\mathcal A(i,j) = T_x\mathfrak X/(K_i+K_j)$ is unchanged. The object the author needs is stronger — a *pre-quotient repacking* $\Phi:E_U\to E_{U'}$, conformal on the canonical null geometry, $\Phi^*\hat g_{U'} = \Omega_\Phi^2\hat g_U$, that also acts on the readout, incidence and connection data, so that $Q_U\to Q_{U'}$, the kernels $K_i(U) = \ker dQ_i^U$ change, and the agreeing structure is tuned. The invariance condition is the author's, stated strongly:

$$q_{U'}\circ\Phi = q_U,\qquad g_G[U'] = g_G[U]$$

— the presentation upstairs changes, the agreeing structure may change, and the gravity quotient does not. **The fork.** In Kerr–Newman, $a$ and $Q$ change the spacetime metric: frame dragging, multipoles, the horizon. So either (A) $a$ and $Q$ live partly in rendering data that the gravity base forgets, or (B) the gravity-base metric changes between microstates and only a deeper quotient is fixed. This theory's Law I decides more than the branch might wish: the gravity base carries Einstein–Maxwell, so it sees a single hole's $M$, $a$ and $Q$, which are therefore not repackable at fixed $g_G$. What *is* repackable is what the gravity base forgets, and the architecture already guarantees one such budget: on the gravity base a source and its twin are one point (the twin theorem), so any exchange of their representational data — the occupancy assignments of §V.4 — is a repacking at fixed $g_G$. The Kerr–Newman simplex therefore parameterizes a hole's gravitationally visible state, and moving on it is a change of the gravity base itself, unless $a$ and $Q$ are shown to descend from rendering data — a construction, not an assumption, and the branch's first real test.

**Kernel transport, and why gauge does not repack [D].** Let $\Phi$ cover the identity on the gravity base and let the readouts be transported with it, $Q_i^{U'} = Q_i^U\circ\Phi^{-1}$. Then $K_i(U') = d\Phi\,K_i(U)$ and $\mathcal A_{U'}(i,j) = d\Phi\,\mathcal A_U(i,j)$: the agreeing structure is carried along, unchanged up to isomorphism. The two structures the theory already has that fix $g_G$ by construction — the $U(1)_{\rm mer}$ gauge transformations, whose quotient *is* the gravity base, and the automorphisms of the $U(1)_H$ bundle — are of this kind. *Gauge does not repack.* The agreeing structure changes only when $\Phi$ acts on the incidence correspondence of §XIII.4 differently from its action on the base, so the repackings are the automorphisms $G = \operatorname{Aut}_{B_{\rm grav} }(\mathcal I)$ of the correspondence over the identity of the gravity base, modulo those that carry the readouts along, $H$. That quotient is a group only if $H$ is normal in $G$; otherwise it is an orbit space, or — more in keeping with the rest of this part — an action groupoid. Whichever it is, if it is trivial the branch is empty, which is its first kill test made concrete.

**The decisive question, answered within the frozen tower [T/V/D].** Does even one non-gauge repacking exist that changes agreeing structure while $q_{U'}\circ\Phi = q_U$ and $g_G[U'] = g_G[U]$? Two facts settle it for the author's conformal implementation. First, the optical geometry is a conformal invariant of the pre-quotient metric: for a static metric $h_{\rm opt} = g_{(3)}/N^2$ is unchanged by $g\to\Omega^2g$, and for a stationary one the Randers data $a_{ij} = g_{ti}g_{tj}/g_{tt}^2-g_{ij}/g_{tt}$ and $b_i = -g_{ti}/g_{tt}$ are unchanged — checked symbolically for an arbitrary $\Omega(x)$ **[V]**. So every optical quantity this document uses — the null histories, their conjugate points and Jacobi kernels, the screen projections and the compatibility relation of §XVII.9, $R_{\rm opt}$ and hence $\delta^2 = -r^4R_{\rm opt}/6$, the identifier — is invariant under conformal transformations of the pre-quotient metric. **A conformal transformation cannot switch a selection, cannot move the Kerr–Newman weights, and cannot change any agreeing structure read from optical data.** The conformal implementation of repacking is empty in the optical layer, and this holds independently of the fork of the previous paragraph. Second, the known symmetries of the frozen tower $E\to\mathcal M\to B_{\rm grav}$ over the identity of the gravity base — the $U(1)_H$ gauge group, the $U(1)_{\rm mer}$ gauge group and the deck transformation $\tau$ of the cover — are of no use: gauge carries the readouts along, and $\tau$ exchanges the two appearances, $K_1\leftrightarrow K_2$, leaving $\mathcal A(1,2) = T/(K_1+K_2)$ unchanged **[D]**. None of the tower's known symmetries at fixed $g_G$ changes agreeing structure; a complete classification of the automorphisms of the incidence correspondence is a larger theorem, and it belongs to the groupoid direction. What does change agreeing structure is *which histories render*: two sheets through one sky point have different lines of sight as kernels (§XVI.4), so switching the rendered member changes $\mathcal A$ — but that is a change of rendering, not of geometry, and it is not a transformation of $E$ at all. So the answer is: **no, as a conformal transformation; yes, as a change of rendering.** The author's budget within an invariant quotient survives exactly as Part XVI's moduli of sheets at fixed $g_G$; the mechanism proposed for moving through it dies; and the Reissner–Nordström and Majumdar–Papapetrou optical results stand as independent discoveries. One door remains, and it is named: a repacking would have to act on data the optical layer does not see — the Hopf fiber lengths, which the locking of Part IV ties to $g_G$, or the meridian scale $\kappa$ of item 9 in §XI.2, which is open — or on the rendering functional itself.

## XVIII.7 Centrade selection, and fields as obstruction [S]

Put a connection $\Theta$ on the bundle $\mathfrak R\to\mathfrak M$ whose fibers are admissible unpackings. If repacking centrades in different orders gives the same result, $\mathcal F_\Theta = 0$; if not, $\mathcal F_\Theta\neq0$ measures the obstruction to settling all choices at once. The two-body budget is then $\mathcal F_\Theta(C_1,C_2)\neq0$ — unpacking $C_1$ then $C_2$ differs from the reverse — which is deeper than a conserved sum imposed by hand, and the author's claim that an electric field is the imprint of conditioning that cannot be settled becomes a target, $F_{\rm EM}\overset{?}{=}$ a representation of the curvature of the centrade-selection connection, stated and not identified. To keep "centrade" from meaning anything localized, a centrade is required to have a localized incidence carrier; at least two incompatible resolution channels; a non-product admissible unpacking space; and a measurable obstruction — curvature, holonomy — when reduction fails. A charged or spinning hole is the principal candidate — the Kerr–Newman simplex gives its constrained state space, but the measurable repacking obstruction has not been constructed; an interaction vertex may be one; a random point is not. One negative result narrows the obstruction test at once: the Weyl connection of the optical conformal factor, $A = d\ln W$, is exact and therefore flat, so by the offloading theorem of §XV.6 it carries no holonomy and cannot be the connection whose curvature is $R_{\rm opt}$; a curvature realization of the nonextremality, if there is one, lives on the repacking bundle and is non-abelian **[D]**.

## XVIII.8 Boltzmannian space [S/O]

Let the physical microstates be the inequivalent repackings, $\mathfrak M_{\rm phys} = \mathscr U_{\rm adm}/\mathcal G_{\rm gauge}$ — gauge copies must not be counted, or the entropy is meaningless — and let a macrostate be a coarse-graining $\Pi$, with $S(\mathcal M) = \log\mu(\Pi^{-1}\mathcal M)$ for a measure $\mu$ the theory must *derive*. The author's Machian claim is then a statement about multiplicity: the macrostate of the Galaxy's bulk has overwhelmingly more compatible microstates than any locally oriented rendering, so conditioning on belonging to it makes the Galactic frame typical without placing it in the laws — and a black hole's fixed, untunable parameters are a remnant of that conditioning, while the laws remain invariant across microstates one does not see. Part XVII.10 supplies a first instance of the shape: the disk normal is an order parameter of a collective sheet, selected by multiplicity over noisy members and by no member alone; whether the sheet's constraint statistics can be read as the measure $\mu$ is open.

## XVIII.9 Why static first [T]

For a static spacetime the optical geometry is a Riemannian metric and "optical Ricci" is the object used above. For Kerr and Kerr–Newman the stationary optical geometry is Randers–Finsler — frame dragging adds a one-form — and the optical Ricci tensor is no longer the same object. The disciplined order is Reissner–Nordström, extremal Reissner–Nordström, Majumdar–Papapetrou, and only then Kerr; the static program is already rich enough. A first stationary test confirms the caution **[V]**: for Kerr–Newman the scalar curvature of the Randers Riemannian part $a_{ij} = g_{ti}g_{tj}/g_{tt}^2-g_{ij}/g_{tt}$, computed numerically with the static formula reproduced to $10^{-7}$ at $a = 0$, is *not* zero at extremality once the hole spins — $-0.04$ at $r = 3M$ for $a = 0.6$, $Q = 0.8$, and $-0.10$ to $-0.14$ for extremal Kerr, against $-0.07$ for Schwarzschild; nor is the Zermelo, or ZAMO, optical metric $g_{(3)}/N^2$ with $N^2 = \Delta\Sigma\sin^2\theta/g_{\phi\phi}$, which comes closer — $-0.014$ against $-0.044$ subextremal at $r = 3M$ — but does not vanish. Nor does the full Randers structure rescue it. The Finsler Ricci scalar ${\rm Ric}(y)$ of the Randers optical geometry was computed from exact spray coefficients, and a chosen diagnostic formed from it — $\bar R_F := 3\langle{\rm Ric}(y)/F(y)^2\rangle$, the $a_{ij}$-sphere-averaged Finsler Ricci, which equals the Riemannian scalar curvature when the one-form vanishes, reproducing $-6/r^4$ to four figures for Schwarzschild and zero for extremal Reissner–Nordström, but which is not a canonical Finsler scalar curvature away from that limit — is not zero at extremality with spin: at $(r,\theta) = (3M,1.0)$ it is $-0.020$ for extremal Kerr–Newman with $a = 0.6$, $-0.036$ for $a = 0.8$, and $-0.056$ for extremal Kerr, against $-0.074$ for Schwarzschild **[V]**. Across the cases tested and at two points the values fit, to about two percent, the empirical relation

$$\bar R_F\simeq-\frac{6}{r^4}\big(\delta^2+c\,a^2\big),\qquad c\approx0.75\ \text{at }(3M,1.0),\quad 0.88\ \text{at }(6M,0.6)$$

— a fit at a few locations with a position-dependent coefficient, not a law. What it shows is definite enough: **in this diagnostic, static charge extremality cancels the Reissner–Nordström trace contribution, whereas spin leaves a residual.** The dictionary "nonextremality is the optical trace" is exact for the static sector and does not carry over to the spinning one, which is itself information for the budget: the charge channel and the spin channel are not on an equal optical footing, as §XVIII.1 cautioned.

## XVIII.10 Kill tests, status, program [D]

**Kill tests.** One is already decided: the *conformal implementation* is retired, since no conformal transformation of the pre-quotient metric changes anything optical (§XVIII.6), while rendering-space repacking remains open — a killed mechanism, not a killed branch. The branch retires further if $a$ and $Q$ cannot be shown to be rendering data, so that the simplex's moves are changes of the gravity base rather than repackings; if the sign carried between the inversion's two appearances cannot be derived; if the microstate measure cannot be derived gauge-free; if the two-centrade non-product constraint does not arise intrinsically — the two-centre Majumdar–Papapetrou test — but only after a macrostate is imposed; or if $R_{\rm opt}$ cannot be constructed as the curvature of a selection connection rather than asserted to be one.
