# PART XX — PROJECTIVE SPIN HISTORIES: THE VERSION-25 BRANCH [T/V/D/S/O]

*An experimental branch on top of the version-24 control. The author distinguishes two kinds of separation: the ordinary separation of two spacetime alternatives, which can be large, small or vanishing, and a projective opening datum — when a pair approaches coincidence its magnitude goes to zero while the manner of approach survives in a resolved space. Part XVII already built exactly this for null histories: at $Y = 0$ the separation has no direction, but on the exceptional divisor of the blow-up the direction $[Y]\in\mathbb P(K_\Xi)$ survives, and the theory learned there that smallness and freedom are not the same thing. The intuition of this part is that matter carries an analogous zero-width directional slot, and that for a spin-$\tfrac12$ particle its rendered state space is the Bloch sphere. Nothing in the frozen architecture is rewritten to make this work; whatever is added is derived from structures the theory already has, with Kerr's construction as the sanity rule.*

## XX.0 The proposal, and its firewalls [S]

Three objects are kept separate from the start: the physical spacetime path $\gamma:\tau\mapsto x(\tau)$ of a massive particle; a zero-width projective opening datum $Y$, $[Y]$, whose magnitude is a separation in whatever history bundle is built and whose direction is what survives as $|Y|\to0$; and the quantum spin ray $[\psi]\in\mathbb{CP}^1$. The thesis is not that these coincide. It is that the theory may possess a canonical map $\mathscr S$ from appropriately equipped history data to $\mathbb{CP}^1$, so that a particle history is provisionally equipped data $(\gamma,[\psi])$ — a spacetime curve and a path in an associated state space, related by transport rather than collapsed into one coordinate.

**Firewall 1: the cosmos supplies the slot, never the verdict.** Canonical geometry may supply the availability and geometry of the spin-state slot, a bundle $\mathscr S_{\rm spin}$ over particle histories; local preparation, fields and measurement select and evolve the section $\tau\mapsto[\psi(\tau)]$. A preferred cosmic orientation appearing in local spin predictions would be a failure. **Firewall 2: the Kerr rule.** The canonical space is not enlarged by a Bloch sphere, a $\mathbb C^2$ or an $S^2$; the theory already owns the Hopf fibration $U(1)\to S^3\to S^2$ as its compact real form, and the first question is whether the spin slot is an associated or readout structure of that geometry. **Firewall 3: the type barrier.** The opening direction of Part XVII is a null-history object, available where an optical conjugate gate opens, and the theory found kernels that vanish at given locations; electron spin is ubiquitous and timelike. So the null $[Y]$ is the prototype and cannot simply be reused; a timelike extension is required. **Firewall 4: phase is not scale.** The split real form's transport is $\mathbb R^\times$ and cannot be renamed a Berry phase.

## XX.1 The standard geometry to be reproduced [T]

A pure spin-$\tfrac12$ state is $\psi\in\mathbb C^2$ with $\psi^\dagger\psi = 1$, a point of $S^3$; the overall phase is unobservable, so the state space is $S^3/U(1) = \mathbb{CP}^1\simeq S^2$ with Bloch vector $\mathbf n = \psi^\dagger\boldsymbol\sigma\psi$. Rotations act through $SU(2)\to SO(3)$: a $2\pi$ rotation sends $\psi\to-\psi$ while $\mathbf n$ returns, and $4\pi$ restores $\psi$. The Berry connection on the ray space is $-i\langle\psi|d\psi\rangle$, with holonomy $-\tfrac12\Omega$ around a loop enclosing solid angle $\Omega$, up to orientation convention. Measurement along $\mathbf m$ gives $P_\pm = (1\pm\mathbf n\cdot\mathbf m)/2$, and for two rays $|\langle\phi|\psi\rangle|^2 = (1+\mathbf n_\phi\cdot\mathbf n_\psi)/2$. Standard path integrals sum over histories, and the Born rule does not by itself postulate a pair of paths; what the theory has is its own dual-history structure, and the unification principle is that when the same doubled algebra recurs one first tests whether it is one structure.

## XX.2 What the theory already owns [V/D]

The complex shell with $SL(2,\mathbb C)$, whose compact real form is the Hopf fibration $U(1)\to SU(2)\simeq S^3\to S^2$ (Parts I, XVII); the connection $U(1)_H$ with the local normalization $\mathcal A_H = \tfrac12(1-\cos\theta)\,d\phi$, $F_H = \tfrac12\sin\theta\,d\theta\wedge d\phi$ (§IX.1); the doubled history and connection architecture with one holomorphic connection whose compact restriction is phase and whose split restriction is scale (Parts XV, XVII); the conjugate lifts $\tilde\gamma_\pm = \gamma\pm iY$; the blow-up and projective opening direction of Part XVII; the twin involution $-1$ on the cover $S^3\to\mathbb{RP}^3$, which is the gravity base (Part 0); the quaternionic structure $j$ as the conjugate lift on shell spinors (§XIX.2d); and the Kerr principle of Part XIX. Nothing is constructed in this section.

## XX.3 The massive-history extension [D]

For a timelike four-velocity $u^a$ the rest space $H_u = \{v:u_av^a = 0\}$ is three-dimensional. A doubled massive history with infinitesimal separation $Y(\tau) = \epsilon N(\tau)$ in $H_u$ has, as $\epsilon\to0$, a vanishing physical separation and a surviving direction. The blow-up must be specified: a neighbouring timelike geodesic carries Jacobi data $(Y,D_uY)\in H_u\oplus H_u$, six components, and blowing up the diagonal of the local Jacobi initial-data space would give $\mathbb P(H_u\oplus H_u)\simeq\mathbb{RP}^5$ (a space of all parametrized histories is infinite-dimensional; the statement is about the local geodesic moduli). The construction here is the *fiberwise blow-up of the spatial-separation bundle*: it retains the instantaneous opening $Y$ and projects away the derivative opening $D_uY$, and its exceptional divisor is, at each instant, $\mathbb P(H_u)\simeq\mathbb{RP}^2$ — the *unoriented* lines of the rest space **[T/D, given that choice]**. Why spin should depend on $Y$ rather than on the complete opening datum $(Y,D_uY)$ is a kill test the branch owes an answer to, not a detail to hide; the natural answer, that the derivative opening is momentum-like data of the history and not state data of the slot, is a proposal **[S]**. Two things distinguish this from the null prototype. It is ubiquitous: along a timelike geodesic the Jacobi fields orthogonal to $u$ form a six-dimensional space, and their instantaneous direction is free in $H_u$, so a kinematic candidate slot exists along every massive history and needs no conjugate gate — kill test 1 passes for the candidate; that this blow-up is the precursor of physical spin is the branch's proposal **[S]**, not a consequence of the geometry. And it is unoriented: $Y\sim-Y$, whereas antipodal Bloch vectors are distinct orthogonal states — the bare map $[Y]\mapsto\mathbf n$ cannot be one-to-one, which is kill test 2. The orientation double cover of the divisor is the sphere of oriented directions, $S(H_u)\simeq S^2\to\mathbb P(H_u)$, and *that sphere, entire, is the candidate slot*: the fiber of possible oriented zero-width directions, not any one of them.

## XX.4 The slot, the spin-group identification, and the orthogonal partner [T/V/D/S]

**The slot is the fiber, not a point of it.** Version 25 first let a particular history's opening $[Y]$, with a lift sign, select a definite Bloch direction; that made the geometry of one doubled history choose the actual spin state, against the first firewall. The cleaner statement is the one the firewall demands: the entire sphere $S(H_u)\simeq S^2$ of oriented zero-width directions is the state space available over the worldline, and local preparation and dynamics choose the section $\tau\mapsto\mathbf n(\tau)$. The chain is

$$H_u\ \longrightarrow\ \mathbb P(H_u)\simeq\mathbb{RP}^2\ \longleftarrow\ S(H_u)\simeq S^2\ \overset{\mathfrak s}{\simeq}\ \mathbb{CP}^1_{\rm shell},$$

and the doubled histories furnish transports and comparisons in this bundle (§XX.6); they do not choose the state.

**The identification is Part I.** The central theorem target — an $SU(2)$-equivariant identification $\mathfrak s:{\rm Spin}(H_u)\leftrightarrow SU(2)_{\rm shell}$ ("soldering" in a loose sense; a solder form proper would identify an internal bundle with a tangent bundle, and no spacetime tetrad is claimed) compatible with $SU(2)\to SO(3)$, the rest-space frame bundle and the Hopf $U(1)$ — turns out to be the theory's own construction. Part I builds the shell as the sphere of fixed radius in the *normal rest space* $N_\gamma$ of the worldline, whose Lorentzian real form is the rest space $H_u$ (§I.1, §I.4); its Hopf lift is $SU(2)\to SU(2)/U(1)\simeq S^2$ with $SU(2)$ the spin cover of the rest space's rotations, "the fiber the stabilizer of the spin-group action" (§I.5), and the fiber acting as the spinor phase (§I.6). In quaternionic form, with ${\rm Im}\,\mathbb H = H_u$: the Hopf map $q\mapsto q\,u\,\bar q$ lands on $S(H_u)$, is invariant under the fiber $q\mapsto qe^{u\chi}$, and left multiplication by $g\in SU(2)_{\rm shell}$ covers the $SO(3)$ rotation of $H_u$, with $g$ and $-g$ the same rotation **[V]**. So ${\rm Spin}(H_u)\simeq SU(2)_{\rm shell}$, $\mathbb{CP}^1_{\rm shell}\simeq S(H_u)$, and $U(1)\to SU(2)\to S(H_u)$ hold *by construction of Part I* **[D]**. Precisely: a spin structure is a lift of the oriented orthonormal frame bundle from $SO(3)$ to ${\rm Spin}(3)$, and the Hopf bundle $U(1)\to SU(2)\to S^2$ is the homogeneous spin-*state* bundle associated with that spin group; what Part I supplies is the local spin-group identification and its associated Hopf–Bloch bundle, and its base sphere is the slot. And with it the zero-width construction is no longer needed to prove that the slot *exists* — Part I gives ${\rm Spin}(H_u)\simeq SU(2)$ and $SU(2)/U(1)\simeq S^2$ outright — so the branch's distinctive claim is subtler: the projective-history machinery may explain why and how massive matter *occupies and transports within* a slot the shell already contains. The division of labor is then clean: the shell gives the kinematic possibility space; the projective-history structure, if it works, its physical realization and transport; local fields the actual state evolution; and $SL(2,\mathbb C)$ the relativistic extension. What remains is only the electron hypothesis — that physical massive matter uses this canonical associated spin bundle **[S]**.

**The orthogonal partner, and the orientation [V/D].** The quaternionic structure $j$ of §XIX.2d, which is the conjugate lift on shell spinors, sends the Bloch vector of $\psi$ to $-\mathbf n$ **[V]**: *the conjugate structure supplies a canonical orthogonal partner to any spin state,* $[\psi]\mapsto[j\psi]$, the involution that exchanges the two states along a line — and that is all it is asked to supply. On orientation, version 25's argument was circular: writing $\gamma\pm iY$ already presumes an oriented $Y$. The exact statement is that an *ordered* doubled history $(\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2)$ carries $Y = \Gamma_2-\Gamma_1$ and so lifts the projective line to its orientation double cover $S^2$, the complex structure packaging that order as the conjugate pair; if the physical pair is unordered, the orientation remains open. Whether the two legs of a pair are the two orthogonal states along the line is a later possibility **[S]**, not a theorem.

**The local sign, and the global twin [D/O].** On the shell, $\psi$ and $-\psi$ have the same Bloch vector **[V]** and are one ray: the shell's antipodal involution is the $2\pi$ spinor sign, exactly, because the shell *is* ${\rm Spin}(H_u)$. Version 25 then identified this with the global twin of Part 0, and that was a category error: $\Sigma_{\rm rep} = S^3(\ell)\to\Sigma_{\rm grav} = \mathbb{RP}^3(\ell)$ is a cover of *positions*, whose antipodal points are two representational locations, while $SU(2)\to SO(3)$ is a cover of *frames*, whose antipodal points are one state at one location. The two covers are isomorphic as $\mathbb Z_2$ covering types; whether the theory canonically identifies their deck transformations requires an intertwining $\Xi:\Sigma_{\rm rep}\to SU(2)$ with $\Xi(-x) = -\Xi(x)$ respecting the projections, and none has been built. That identification is open, and it is a striking target rather than a result.

**XX.4a The Kerr–fermion directional-slot dictionary [T/V/D/S/O].** Parts XVII and XX each built an $\mathbb{RP}^2$ over a source worldline, and the question the author raised is whether they are the same space. They are, canonically. Part XVII's split family is the conjugacy orbit $\sigma_{[N]} = R\sigma_0R^{-1}$ with $\sigma_0 = \tilde\jmath_0\circ\rho$, $\tilde\jmath_0$ the lifted reflection through the plane $N_0^\perp$, so its labelling by unoriented lines is intrinsic — a reflection determines its plane, the plane its normal line — and needs no base point; Part XX's $\mathbb P(H_u)$ is the space of unoriented lines of the same rest space $H_u$, on which the same shell $SU(2)$ acts through $SO(3)$. Two levels must be kept apart. On the fundamental shell carrier the compact real form *induces* the quaternionic structure $j$, $j^2 = -1$ — Part XVII's $\rho$ is the group involution $\rho(g) = (g^\dagger)^{-1}$, $\rho^2 = 1$, and the two are not the same kind of object. Composing the carrier action of $j$ with the lifted reflection gives the carrier-level lifted reflection $\hat\sigma_N = (i\boldsymbol\sigma\cdot\mathbf N)\circ j$, which squares to $+1$ and obeys $\hat\sigma_{-N} = -\hat\sigma_N$: the two carrier-level lifts of one reflection differ by the central sign (no claim about the full spacetime Pin extension, which remains open, is made here). The real-form involution itself is what the lift induces by conjugation, $\sigma_{[N]}(g) = \hat\sigma_N\,g\,\hat\sigma_N^{-1}$, and there the sign cancels, $\sigma_{[-N]} = \sigma_{[N]}$ — which is precisely what makes $[N]\mapsto\sigma_{[N]}$ well-defined on the unoriented line. Conjugation by the shell is equivariant, $U\hat\sigma_NU^{-1} = \pm\hat\sigma_{R(U)N}$, hence $\sigma_{[R(U)N]}$ exactly at the involution level; and the stabilizer of $\sigma_{[N]}$ under conjugation is exactly the stabilizer $O(2)$ of the line — all checked on hundreds of random shell elements and on the two generators of $O(2)$ **[V]**. So $N\leftrightarrow-N$ changes the carrier lift by the central sign but not the split-real-form selector. Orientation becomes meaningful again only after passing to the oriented cover, where $N$ and $-N$ are distinct directional states or order parameters: $N,-N\in S(H_u)$ are distinct while $[N] = [-N]\in\mathbb P(H_u)$ — the central sign of the lift and the distinction of the two oriented directions are related bookkeeping, not the same statement. So

$$\Phi:\ \mathbb P(H_u)\longrightarrow\mathcal R_{\rm split},\qquad [N]\longmapsto\sigma_{[N]},$$

is an $SO(3)$-equivariant bijection between two orbits with the same stabilizer, and it is the *only* $SO(3)$-equivariant one — set-theoretic bijections between two copies of $\mathbb{RP}^2$ abound, and the theorem is meaningful precisely because the uniqueness is equivariant: equivariant bijections between them differ by the normalizer $N_{SO(3)}(O(2))/O(2)$, which is trivial because an element conjugating the $O(2)$ of a line into itself preserves that line **[T/V]**. The diagram

$$\mathbb{CP}^1_{\rm shell}\simeq S(H_u)\longrightarrow\mathbb P(H_u)\overset{\Phi}{\simeq}\mathcal R_{\rm split}$$

is therefore canonical from the existing constructions, the first map forgetting orientation, with no extra axis and no coordinate **[D]**. **The compact and split branches share one local directional skeleton** — an oriented direction $S(H_u)$ together with its unoriented quotient $\mathbb P(H_u)$, read at different levels by different physics. Read through the split family, a line $[N]$ labels the real structure $\sigma_{[N]}$; for Kerr, $[N] = [J]$ selects $\sigma_{[J]}$ and hence the equatorial common-real plane in which the Newman–Janis displacement of §XIX.2b lies, and the axial datum $J$ orients the line, so that $\hat J = J/|J|$ lies on the orientation cover $S(H_u)\simeq\mathbb{CP}^1_{\rm shell}$. Read through the compact form, that orientation cover is the spin-state sphere of §XX.4. Kerr's intrinsic angular-momentum direction and the electron's Bloch direction thus occupy two readings of one shell-derived directional slot — split and unoriented for real-form selection, compact and oriented for the local spin-state geometry.

The representation firewall is kept: *same directional slot $\neq$ same physical representation.* For the electron the point of $S(H_u)$ is a quantum spin ray in the fundamental spin-$\tfrac12$ representation, free to evolve under local dynamics; for Kerr, $J$ is a classical axial order parameter of the solution whose projective class selects a real-form member. A Kerr black hole is not a Bloch state, and no half-integer spin is inferred for it. The identification $\Phi$ is derived **[D/V]**; reading the common slot as a physical Kerr–fermion unification is a proposal **[S]**; and any claim that the electron and Kerr are two regimes of one dynamical object, or that finite history separation converts quantum spin into classical Kerr-like angular momentum, is open **[O]** — it is the question of §XX.12, not an answer to it.

## XX.5 The Berry test [V]

Pulling the shell's connection back along the candidate map, the question is whether the existing $\mathcal A_H$ *is* the Berry connection with the spin-$\tfrac12$ normalization. In the north gauge $\psi = (\cos\tfrac\theta2,\ e^{i\phi}\sin\tfrac\theta2)$, $-i\langle\psi|d\psi\rangle = \sin^2\tfrac\theta2\,d\phi = \tfrac12(1-\cos\theta)\,d\phi = \mathcal A_H$ exactly **[V]**. For the latitude loop $\theta = \theta_0$, $\phi:0\to2\pi$, $\oint\mathcal A_H = \pi(1-\cos\theta_0) = \Omega/2$ with $\Omega = 2\pi(1-\cos\theta_0)$ **[V]**. No coefficient was adjusted; the normalization of §IX.1 is the spin-$\tfrac12$ one. Two qualifications. This does not prove the electron's state is the shell's sphere; it proves the theory already contains a Hopf connection with precisely the spin-$\tfrac12$ Berry normalization. And it is exact for the *canonical monopole* representative: Part IX leaves local deformations of $\mathcal A_H$ unfixed beyond the unit Chern class, so the identification is a constraint on them, best stated at the curvature level: $\iota_{\rm spin}^*F_H\overset{!}{=}\tfrac12\sin\theta\,d\theta\wedge d\phi$, with the connection matching modulo gauge. Components of the older connection along time or the meridian need not vanish globally; restricted to a pure spin-state sphere they contribute nothing. The dangerous pieces are deformations *tangent to the sphere*, and it is precisely the previously underdetermined angular part of $\mathcal A_H$ that the spin identification would fix. Kill test 4: the canonical Hopf component passes exactly; the full-connection pullback remains to be proved. Kill test 7 passes: no second phase field is needed. If the identification holds while this pullback constraint fixes the otherwise underdetermined connection, the branch feeds a constraint back into the older architecture, which would be more than a resemblance.

## XX.6 Two histories and relative phase [V/D/S]

Berry's phase needs one cyclic state evolution, not two paths; but the theory's doubled histories give a natural comparison. Two admissible alternatives carrying lifts $[\psi_1(\tau)]$, $[\psi_2(\tau)]$ with agreeing endpoints close to a loop $C = \psi_1\circ\psi_2^{-1}$, and $\Delta\gamma = \oint_C\mathcal A_H = \int_{\Sigma_C}F_H$: *individual histories carry transport; comparisons of histories carry holonomy* — the style of Parts XIV–XV. Explicitly, for the two paths "meridian down, latitude across" and "latitude across, meridian down" between $(\theta_1,0)$ and $(\theta_2,\Delta\phi)$, the analytic holonomy is $\tfrac12\Delta\phi(\cos\theta_1-\cos\theta_2) = \Omega/2$ **[V]**, and the discretized Pancharatnam product over four thousand steps gives $\mp0.74661$ against $\Omega/2 = 0.74661$ **[V]**, the sign being the orientation convention. The question that is the theory's rather than the textbook's — which piece of the dual-history data attaches which state path to which spacetime history — has a first answer from §XX.4: for a pair $(\Gamma_1,\Gamma_2)$ the separation $Y$ is one vector field, pointing from $\Gamma_1$ toward $\Gamma_2$ along the first and oppositely along the second, so an ordered pair *could* assign $\mathbf n(\tau)$ to $\Gamma_1$ and $-\mathbf n(\tau)$ to $\Gamma_2$ — orthogonal spin paths with opposite Berry phases, whose comparison doubles the geometric phase. That the two histories of a physical pair carry those two orthogonal states is a proposal **[S]**, not a derivation: it would restrict nearby histories to antipodal spin pairs, where ordinary local dynamics allows arbitrary nearby states, and it depends on the identification the branch admits is open. The safe result is the one of §XX.4 — the conjugate structure supplies a canonical orthogonal partner — and the doubled-phase rule is recorded as the compact sibling of Part XV's doubled scale character **[S]**, a structural rhyme, not an identity; the doubled construction is a candidate implementation of comparative phase, not a replacement for ordinary unitary spin evolution.

## XX.7 The rotation and Born tests [V]

With $U_z(\varphi) = e^{-i\varphi\sigma_z/2}$ acting through the shell's $SU(2)_{\rm left}$, $U_z(2\pi)\psi = -\psi$ and $U_z(4\pi)\psi = \psi$ while the Bloch vector returns at $2\pi$ **[V]** — the state lives on the cover, and the base $S^2$ alone would have produced a vector (kill test 5 passes). On the weight-two carrier of Part XIX, $(U_z(2\pi)\psi)^{\odot2} = +\psi^{\odot2}$ **[V]**: the same projective base, an integer-spin weight, return at $2\pi$ — §XX.10's comparison, already in hand. And with the ordinary Hermitian structure on $\mathbb C^2$, $|\langle\phi|\psi\rangle|^2 = (1+\mathbf n_\phi\cdot\mathbf n_\psi)/2$ **[V]**: the slot reproduces, and does not deform, spin probabilities (kill test 6 passes); no claim is made to derive the Born rule.

## XX.8 Toward the Dirac extension [T/V/D/S]

Spin-$\tfrac12$ under boosts is the second stage, and its first requirement is kinematic: the rest-frame $\mathbb C^2$ must transform under the shell's $SL(2,\mathbb C)$ so that composed boosts produce the Wigner rotation. Composing boosts of rapidity $(0.8,0,0)$ and $(0,0.6,0.3)$ in $SL(2,\mathbb C)$ and polar-decomposing, $B(v_2)B(v_1) = UP$ with $U\in SU(2)$ the Wigner rotation of angle $0.2445$; its induced $SO(3)$ matrix equals the rotation extracted at the vector level from the Lorentz matrices, and the rest-frame Bloch vector rotates by exactly it **[V]** — a check that the group was implemented consistently, and the statement it supports is that the existing shell group has exactly the required kinematics once it is identified with the particle's spin bundle; the transport itself needs the worldline bundle and connection (kill test 9 passes at the kinematic level, conditionally on that identification). The Weyl sectors come from existing indices: the Cartan twist $g\mapsto(g^\dagger)^{-1}$, whose fixed points are the compact real form $SU(2)$, sends a boost to its inverse and fixes rotations **[V]**, so the $(0,\tfrac12)$ representation can be represented as the fundamental $(\tfrac12,0)$ composed with the compact real structure $\rho$ — algebraically exact **[T]**. That the theory's geometric conjugate lift is physically the chirality operation, so that $S_D = S_L\oplus S_R$ is the fundamental of the complex shell plus its $\rho$-twist, is conditional **[S/D]**: it introduces a *derived chirality index* — a representation label — but no new canonical coordinate, which is exactly the author's stacking of indices. **The local dynamics test, first stage [V].** The decisive test is no longer topological: does the associated spin connection transport spin as physics does, with no extra term? The kinematic half can be run on the shell's group. Fermi–Walker transport of a torque-free rest-frame spinor along a circular orbit — pure boosts in the momentary *transported* frame, composed in $SL(2,\mathbb C)$ over twenty thousand steps — returns after one orbit with residual boost $10^{-12}$ and a holonomy rotation about the orbital axis of exactly $2\pi(\gamma-1)$, retrograde, at $v = 0.3$, $0.6$ and $0.9$ **[V]**: the Thomas precession, with nothing added. The dynamical half is the local Hamiltonian's by the first firewall — Larmor precession comes from $H_{\rm spin}$, not from the slot — and its geometric residue, the phase of a spin in a slowly rotating field, is the Hopf holonomy of §XX.5. What remains of the test is the one piece the connection could add: the components of $\mathcal A_H$ *along the worldline*, which restricted to the state sphere contribute nothing but along the history give a phase, and which must be shown to be pure gauge or identified with the dynamical phase — the transport form of the pullback constraint of §XX.5 **[O]**.

**The bundle over the mass shell [D/S].** The $Y$-versus-$(Y,D_uY)$ question of §XX.3 points at the relativistic architecture: the four-velocity belongs to the *base*, the spin ray to the *fiber*. Let $\mathcal P_m = \{(x,p)\in T^*B_{\rm rep}: g_{\rm rep}^{-1}(p,p) = -m^2,\ p\ \text{future-directed}\}$, the future mass shell in the cotangent bundle, identified through the musical isomorphism of $g_{\rm rep}$ and $u = p^\sharp/m$ with the future unit-timelike tangent bundle of the representational spacetime, since matter and detectors live on $B_{\rm rep}$ (Law IV) and the mass-shell condition uses its physical metric; deriving that metric from $g_G$ is the open bridge of Part IV, so the global $\mathcal P_m$ is conditional on it while its local Lorentzian comparison limit is unambiguous. The candidate spin bundle is $\mathcal S_{\rm spin}\to\mathcal P_m$ with fiber $\mathbb{CP}^1_{\rm shell}\simeq S(H_u)$ over $(x,u)$, with the exact tangent sequence of the bundle $\pi:\mathcal S_{\rm spin}\to\mathcal P_m$,

$$0\longrightarrow V\mathcal S_{\rm spin}\longrightarrow T\mathcal S_{\rm spin}\xrightarrow{\ d\pi\ }\pi^*T\mathcal P_m\longrightarrow0,\qquad V\mathcal S_{\rm spin}\simeq T\mathbb{CP}^1\ \text{fiberwise},$$

which splits as $H\oplus V$ only once a connection is chosen — and that connection is constructed in §XX.8a. The decomposition organizes the kill test of §XX.3 without deriving it away: $Y/|Y|$ is the candidate state-slot direction, $u$ and $\delta u\sim D_uY$ belong naturally to the base kinematics, and a change in the base induces a Wigner rotation of the fiber, which is exactly what the two computations above exhibit **[D]**; the physical identification of the $Y$ sector with spin, and hence the occupation of the bundle, remain **[S/O]** — the status table is right to keep them open.

**XX.8a The connection on $\mathcal S_{\rm spin}\to\mathcal P_m$, constructed, and the benchmark [T/V/D].** The connection is forced by structures already in hand, and it is the choice that splits the tangent sequence above. Along $x$ at fixed $u$ the horizontal lift is the spin lift of the Levi-Civita connection of $g_{\rm rep}$ — the connection any spinor bundle over a spin manifold carries. Along $u$ at fixed $x$ the base is the velocity hyperboloid $H^3 = SL(2,\mathbb C)/SU(2)$, a homogeneous space of the complex shell's own group, and the canonical connection of that symmetric space has horizontal distribution the pure boosts, $\mathfrak p = i\,\mathfrak{su}(2)$ — the $-1$ eigenspace of the compact real structure $\rho$, the imaginary directions of Part XV. No further choice enters. The horizontal lift of a worldline $(x(\tau),u(\tau))$ is then Fermi–Walker transport of the rest-frame spinor **[T]**, which is what §XX.8's Thomas computation already exercised: the frame carried by successive pure boosts in the transported frame is the horizontal lift. The matter term is the local Hamiltonian's, by the first firewall: minimally coupled Dirac gives in the rest frame the Pauli precession $\boldsymbol\Omega = -(g/2)\,q\,\mathbf B_{\rm rest}$ with $g = 2$ for bare Dirac **[T]**, $\mathbf B_{\rm rest}$ the field in the transported frame, and the spinor turning by $e^{-i\boldsymbol\Omega\cdot\boldsymbol\sigma\,d\tau/2}$.

**The benchmark [V].** In a uniform magnetic field, on cyclotron orbits at $v = 0.3$, $0.6$ and $0.8$ and two charges, the rest-frame spin's turn relative to the velocity after one period from the construction — horizontal lift plus matter term, and nothing else — was compared with the same observable integrated from the Bargmann–Michel–Telegdi equation, for $g = 0$, $1$, $2$, $2.5$ and the observed $2.00232$. The two agree in every case to $5\times10^{-5}$ degrees per period, the discretization error: at $g = 0$ the construction gives pure Thomas, $+17.38^\circ$, $+90^\circ$, $-120^\circ$, as BMT does; at $g = 2$ the spin stays locked to the velocity, as minimal Dirac coupling requires; at $g = 2.00232$ it turns by $-0.44^\circ$, $-0.52^\circ$, $-0.70^\circ$, the anomalous precession, as BMT does **[V]**. Two conventions had to be aligned and carry no physics: the precession sense must match the cyclotron sense of the charge, and the orbit sense must be the one the Lorentz force gives. **No extra term appears.** And none can come from the theory's own connection at fixed state: in the semi-free local model $\mathbb R_t\times S^1\times\mathbb C^2$ of §II the Hopf connection lives on the $\mathbb C^2$ factor and has no component along $\mathbb R_t$, so along a worldline at fixed spin state it contributes neither precession nor phase, its angular part is the Berry connection of §XX.5, and its deformations are constrained by the pullback condition there **[D, on the local model]**. So the bundle built from the theory's existing geometry has the correct state space (§XX.4), the correct Lorentz transport (Wigner, Thomas), the correct geometric phase (§XX.5), and — with the interaction supplied by the matter Hamiltonian and the geometry adding nothing — the correct local spin dynamics of a massive spin-$\tfrac12$ degree of freedom. That is the threshold the branch set itself, crossed for the associated bundle; what remains is the hypothesis that physical matter occupies it.

**XX.8b $\mathbb C^2_{\rm rest}\to S_L\oplus S_R$: the Dirac equation as the identity of the lift [T/V/D].** *Conventions, fixed once:* metric $(+,-,-,-)$; $\sigma^\mu = (1,\boldsymbol\sigma)$, $\bar\sigma^\mu = (1,-\boldsymbol\sigma)$; $p\cdot\sigma := p_\mu\sigma^\mu = p^0-\mathbf p\cdot\boldsymbol\sigma$ and $p\cdot\bar\sigma = p^0+\mathbf p\cdot\boldsymbol\sigma$; $\gamma^\mu$ in the Weyl basis with off-diagonal blocks $\sigma^\mu$, $\bar\sigma^\mu$; the pure boost $B(u) = e^{\xi\,\boldsymbol\sigma\cdot\hat n/2}$ with $\tanh\xi = v$; and $\Lambda(g)$ read *actively*, defined by $g^\dagger\sigma^\mu g = \Lambda^\mu{}_\nu\sigma^\nu$, so that $\Lambda(B)$ carries the rest four-velocity to $(\gamma,\gamma\mathbf v)$. The horizontal lift of §XX.8a from the rest frame to velocity $u$ is the pure boost $B(u)$, and its Cartan twist is $B(u)^{-1}$. Carry one rest-frame spinor $\xi$ — a point of the shell over $(x,u)$ — into both Weyl sectors by the two:

$$u(p) = \sqrt m\,\big(B(u)^{-1}\xi,\ B(u)\,\xi\big)\in S_L\oplus S_R.$$

For a pure boost $B^2 = (p\cdot\bar\sigma)/m$ and $B^{-2} = (p\cdot\sigma)/m$ **[V]**, so $(p\cdot\sigma)\psi_R = m\psi_L$ and $(p\cdot\bar\sigma)\psi_L = m\psi_R$ hold identically, which is $(\gamma\cdot p-m)u(p) = 0$ **[V]**: **the positive-energy momentum-space Dirac constraint is an identity of the boost lift** — both Weyl components are one rest-frame spinor carried by the pure boost and by its Cartan twist. The Cartan-related pair supplies precisely the representation content that the Lorentz-invariant Dirac mass coupling $m(\psi_L^\dagger\psi_R+{\rm h.c.})$ requires; its coefficient $m$ remains physical input, and the existence of the second sector makes the coupling possible without generating it. The bilinears are the standard ones, $\bar uu = 2m$, $\bar u\gamma^\mu u = 2p^\mu$ **[V]**. The construction is Lorentz-covariant with no further input, and the block order matters: on the displayed pair $(B^{-1}\xi,\ B\xi)$ the representation is $D(g) = (g^\dagger)^{-1}\oplus g$, with the vector representation defined by $g^\dagger\sigma^\mu g = \Lambda^\mu{}_\nu\sigma^\nu$; then $\Lambda(B)$ is the boost from rest to $+v$, $D(B)(\xi,\xi) = (B^{-1}\xi,B\xi) = u(p)$, and $D(g)$ carries solutions at $p$ to solutions at $\Lambda(g)p$ for general $g$ **[V]** — one displayed block transforms by the Cartan twist of the other, exactly, and the chirality label of each block is fixed by its representation type, not by its position in the tuple. What has been derived is the on-shell spinor solution structure; the spacetime field equation $(i\gamma^\mu\nabla_\mu-m)\Psi = 0$ is reached from it by the standard reconstruction of a local Lorentz-covariant linear field law with this plane-wave content — legitimate, but a reconstruction step and not a consequence of the boost identity — and minimal coupling is its interacting form, whose semiclassical limit is the BMT dynamics with $g = 2$ verified in §XX.8a **[T]**. The derived chirality index of §XX.8 is now concrete: the two sectors are the two lifts, related by $\rho$.

**The $v$-spinor sector, and completeness [V].** The negative-frequency spinors come from the same architecture. With $\eta = j\xi$ the $j$-conjugated rest spinor and the same lift applied *with opposite sign between the sectors*,

$$v(p) = \sqrt m\,\big(B(u)^{-1}\,j\xi,\ -B(u)\,j\xi\big),\qquad (\gamma\cdot p+m)\,v(p) = 0,$$

and this $v$ is exactly $M_C u(p)^*$ with $M_C = -i\gamma^2$ **[V]**: charge conjugation of the lifted spinor is the lift of the conjugate rest spinor with the graded sign. Over a rest-frame basis $\xi_1,\xi_2$ the completeness relations $\sum_su_s\bar u_s = \gamma\cdot p+m$ and $\sum_sv_s\bar v_s = \gamma\cdot p-m$ hold, with $\bar uu = 2m$, $\bar vv = -2m$ and $\bar uv = 0$ **[V]**. The typing must be exact: for future-directed $p$, $u_s(p)\in\ker(\gamma\cdot p-m)$ while $v_s(p)\in\ker(\gamma\cdot p+m) = \ker(\gamma\cdot(-p)-m)$, so the $v$'s are not further solutions of the same momentum-space equation at $p$; they are the negative-frequency partners, $v_s(p)e^{+ip\cdot x}$ against $u_s(p)e^{-ip\cdot x}$, and the clean object is the paired mode space

$$\mathscr H^{\rm free}_p = \big[\ker(\gamma\cdot p-m)\,e^{-ip\cdot x}\big]\oplus\big[\ker(\gamma\cdot p+m)\,e^{+ip\cdot x}\big],\qquad p^0>0,$$

four independent free plane-wave modes parameterized by one future-directed $p$: **the complete free Dirac plane-wave spinor data, positive- and negative-frequency.** This is not a product of three independent two-state indices: chirality is not a further degeneracy, since each $u$ or $v$ is a bispinor whose two Weyl components are *constrained* by the Dirac relation, and the $u$–$v$ distinction is a mode-family distinction. That is the author's stacking of indices in its exact form — representation packaging with constraints, from one rest-frame $\mathbb C^2$ slot, with no new canonical coordinate. Until quantization is supplied the $v$ family is the negative-frequency sector; canonical quantization reinterprets those modes as antiparticles, and the theory has not derived that step. What is not derived is the occupation — that the theory's matter on $B_{\rm rep}$ is a Dirac field valued in this bundle — nor the quantization and statistics; these stay the branch's hypotheses **[S/O]**.

**The counterfactual meridian-KK transport test [V].** The frozen architecture does *not* make the meridian an electromagnetic Kaluza–Klein circle: Law IV keeps Maxwell as a separate field on $B_{\rm rep}$, Structure III's connection is $\mathcal A_H$ on the Hopf circle and not on the meridian, and the charge branch (§X.10) examined meridian momentum as charge and declined it for three costs — a dilaton, the relocation of Maxwell to the meridian connection, and a free oriented circle action the meridian pencil does not possess. The test here therefore imposes that unadopted geometrization *temporarily and as a counterfactual*: the ansatz $G_{\rm KK} = \eta+\kappa^2(dw+A)^2$ with meridian momentum $p_w = \kappa^2(\dot w+A\cdot\dot x)$ identified with charge, solely to ask whether such a geometrization could also carry spin transport. With a uniform magnetic field and a horizontal spin vector orthogonal to the velocity, integrated over one cyclotron period against the Bargmann–Michel–Telegdi law with the same observable — the rest-frame spin's turn relative to the velocity — three things happen. (i) The raw five-dimensional parallel transport does not preserve a four-dimensional spin: the spin leaks into the meridian direction, its vertical part growing to $\sim0.45$ per period **[V]** — a spin vector in the middle space is not a four-dimensional object. (ii) The transport *induced on the horizontal bundle*, which preserves the spin's norm and orthogonality to $10^{-12}$, turns the spin relative to the velocity by exactly half a revolution per period at every speed and every charge — which is BMT with $g = 1$ only as $v\to0$ and is not a BMT law for any fixed $g$ at finite speed **[V]**. (iii) Exactly when the five-dimensional geodesic is null, charge equal to $\kappa$, the raw transport reproduces the BMT law with $g = 1$ to four figures at three speeds and two values of $\kappa$; off the null case it does not **[V]**. The value $g = 1$ has standard precedent — classic Kaluza–Klein analysis finds $g = 1$ for massive spinning Kaluza–Klein modes **[T]** — and it is half the Dirac baseline $g = 2$ of minimally coupled spin-$\tfrac12$, which underlies BMT before radiative corrections. So the moral is sound and narrow: **the tested minimal Kaluza–Klein Levi-Civita meridian transport does not reproduce Dirac spin dynamics.** This is an independent second failure of the meridian-KK candidate, not a calculation of the theory's adopted electromagnetic connection, and it sharpens the division of labor: the shell geometry gives the spin-state space, the Lorentz and rest-space connection gives Wigner and Thomas transport, and the electromagnetic Hamiltonian gives the magnetic-moment and BMT dynamics — one structure per job, in the spirit of the Kerr principle, which uses existing structure where it produces the right mathematics and refuses to force it into a role with the wrong coefficient. What the test proves is that *the minimal KK, Levi-Civita meridian transport is excluded*; it does not prove that every conceivable meridian-dependent contribution to spin transport vanishes. Any surviving meridian-dependent spin term must independently reproduce BMT and is presently unsupported. The firewall the branch adopts as a constraint to test is $\nabla^{\rm phys}_{\rm spin} = \nabla^{\rm rest}_{\rm spin}+$ the local interaction Hamiltonian, with no universal meridian-geometric precession; it becomes derived only when all symmetry-compatible additional meridian terms are classified and shown to violate BMT **[S/O]**. And one precision for the eventual test: $g_{\rm Dirac} = 2$ is the baseline, the observed $g_e = 2(1+a_e)$ carrying the radiative anomaly; this stage asks only that the geometry not spoil the BMT law supplied by the chosen local Hamiltonian, so the bare test sets $g = 2$ and a later one may feed in the physical $g$ and ask whether anything is added. Derived, as of 25.7: the positive-energy on-shell Dirac spinor relation and the negative-frequency $v$ sector with completeness (§XX.8b). Still open: the theory's matter action, the spacetime Dirac field equation as a dynamical law rather than a reconstruction, the occupation of the negative-energy sector, and quantization with statistics **[S/O]**. A fixed-momentum electron has a two-dimensional spin space; the four components are the covariant packaging, not four Bloch degrees of freedom.

## XX.9 The discrete symmetries and the theory's two spinor structures [T/V/D/S]

On $S_D = S_L\oplus S_R$ the theory has exactly two structures beyond the group: the compact real structure $\rho$, whose induced Dirac intertwiner exchanges the two sectors, and the quaternionic structure $j$ of §XIX.2d, the conjugate lift, acting on each sector. Write the standard operators in the Weyl basis with matrices separated from complex conjugation: $M_P = \gamma^0$; $M_T = \gamma^1\gamma^3$ with $\mathcal T = M_TK$; $M_C = -i\gamma^2$ with $\mathcal C = M_CK$. Then **[V]**:

- the **internal parity matrix** $\gamma^0$ is the linear intertwiner between the two associated Weyl representations, $P_\rho D(g)P_\rho^{-1} = D(\rho(g))$ with $P_\rho = \gamma^0$ and $\rho(g) = (g^\dagger)^{-1}$ the Cartan involution of the group **[V]** — $\rho$ is the involution, $\gamma^0$ the intertwiner it induces, and they are not the same object;
- **time reversal** $\mathcal T$ is $j$ on each sector, up to phase — the quaternionic structure supplies the antilinear spinor operation with the representation properties of time reversal, and the physical $T$ additionally acts on the spacetime and momentum base;
- **charge conjugation** acts as $\mathcal C\psi = (j\psi_R,\ -j\psi_L)$: the conjugate lift after the sector exchange, *with a relative minus sign between the chiral blocks* — $\mathcal C = j_{\rm graded}\circ\rho$, where $j_{\rm graded}$ carries the chirality grading. Plain $j\circ\rho$ would give $(j\psi_R,j\psi_L)$ and is not charge conjugation.

Their actions are verified, not defined, on the coupled equation: $M_C\gamma^{\mu*}M_C^{-1} = -\gamma^\mu$ for every $\mu$, so $\psi^c$ obeys the Dirac equation with $e\to-e$ and the same $m$ — the charge reversed, the mass kept; $\gamma^0\gamma^\mu\gamma^0 = \gamma_\mu$ and $M_T\gamma^{\mu*}M_T^{-1} = \gamma_\mu$ for parity and time reversal **[V]**; $\mathcal C^2 = 1$, $M_P^2 = 1$, $\mathcal T^2 = -1$ **[V]**. The combined operator is *not* a phase, and version 25.6's "$CPT = -1$ with $j^2$ supplying the sign" is **retracted**: with these matrices the internal c-number product is $M_C\,M_P^*\,M_T^* = -\gamma^5$ **[V]**, proportional to the chiral grading. For the c-number spinor maps used here the two $K$'s cancel, so the composite is *linear*, $\mathcal C\mathcal P\mathcal T:\psi(x)\mapsto-\gamma^5\psi(-x)$ up to phase, the spacetime part sending $x\to-x$; the quantum-field $CPT$ operator, with its field-operator and antiunitarity structure, is a different object and is not derived here. The chirality grading $\gamma^5$ is essential and cannot be collapsed into the quaternionic sign — which is also why the ungraded $j\circ\rho$ was wrong; the two errors are one.

Two further limits. The $\rho$-sector exchange reproduces the internal Weyl-sector intertwiner that parity requires; the full spacetime parity transformation $(t,\mathbf x)\mapsto(t,-\mathbf x)$ is an action on the base that needs the Pin extension, which remains open, as the status table says. And a caution on c-number checks: $\mathcal C$ carries a positive-energy spinor to a negative-energy one, $\bar uu = 2m\to-2m$, so the charge reversal is the statement about the coupled equation above, not the sign of a bilinear.

**The meridian intuition, tested [D, conditional].** Part XV found that the exchange $K\to-K$ is $\rho_E$ on $\mathbb C^\times$, and §V.4 that the twin reverses the meridian's orientation. On the Dirac spinor $\rho$ is the sector exchange — the internal factor of *parity*, not charge conjugation, and charge conjugation needs in addition the graded conjugate lift. So **the meridian-sign intuition does not survive as charge conjugation itself**: the simple identification fails. At most, one of the algebraic factors entering the charge-conjugation operator is represented by the same compact-real-structure operation that appears in the meridian sector; whether that reflects a physical common origin is open **[O]**. Nothing was defined as a momentum reversal; the transformation was read off the theory's two structures and its induced action checked (kill test 11 passes for the associated bundle). The Galactic-plane reflection of Part XIII remains distinct from $P$, and the identification of the theory's $\rho$ and $j$ with the physical operators is exact representation theory whose physical force depends on the occupation hypothesis **[S]**.

## XX.10 The integer-spin comparison [V]

A two-state polarization system also has a projective sphere, so a sphere does not determine spin; the representation under rotations does. The theory realizes the distinction as *same projective base, different associated weight*: the fundamental returns at $4\pi$ with $-1$ at $2\pi$, the weight-two carrier ${\rm Sym}^2\mathbb C^2$ returns at $2\pi$ **[V]** (§XX.7). This is Part XIX's weight-two observation read as a spin statement — a consistency clue, not a claim that the centrade carrier is a photon or that its budget states are polarizations.

| **proposed [S]** | the zero-width massive datum as the precursor of spin; physical massive matter using the canonical associated spin bundle — a Dirac field on $B_{\rm rep}$ valued in it; $\mathcal A_H$ as the physical Berry phase on it; the two legs of a pair as orthogonal states; the roles now separated: $\rho$ relating the two Weyl representation sectors, $j$ the quaternionic orthogonal-partner and time-reversal structure, $j_{\rm graded}\circ\rho$ charge conjugation on the Dirac pair; dual histories as comparative phase |
| **open** | the identification of a doubled massive history with an electron's data; why the slot depends on $Y$ and not on $(Y,D_uY)$; the classification of symmetry-compatible meridian-dependent spin terms, on which the no-meridian firewall depends; the worldline components of the Hopf connection beyond the local model; the full-connection pullback and the worldline components of $\mathcal A_H$ as pure gauge or dynamical phase; parity through the Pin extension; the intertwining $\Xi$ of the global twin cover with the spin cover; the occupation itself; the spacetime Dirac equation as a dynamical law rather than a reconstruction; quantization and statistics; full spacetime parity and $CPT$ through the Pin and base actions; a physical common origin, if any, of the $\rho$ in charge conjugation and the $\rho_E$ of the meridian sector; the dual-history pair against arbitrary local spin dynamics |

**Kill tests, with their status.** (1) No ubiquitous massive slot — a ubiquitous kinematic candidate exists; its physical identification is open. (2) Only an unoriented line — the orientation cover $S(H_u)$ is the slot, and an ordered pair lifts to it; for an unordered physical pair, open. (3) Preferred cosmic orientation — passes: no global direction enters. (4) Wrong Hopf normalization — the canonical component passes exactly; the full-connection pullback is open. (5) Wrong rotation representation — passes on the shell. (6) Wrong probabilities — passes. (7) Duplicate phase field — passes: none needed. (8) Phase–scale confusion — respected. (9) Lorentz failure — passes for the associated bundle: the constructed connection's worldline transport is Fermi–Walker, and with the matter term it reproduces BMT for every $g$; the minimal KK meridian transport is excluded as a term of the physical spin's dynamics, as a counterfactual; the dynamical transport awaits the identification and the worldline components of the connection. (10) Coordinate inflation — respected: the chirality index is the two lifts, no new coordinate. (11) Discrete symmetry by definition — passes for the associated bundle: the internal $C$, $P$, $T$ matrices are read off $\rho$ and $j$ with the chirality grading and their induced actions verified; the spacetime parts are open. **The deciding question** — does the existing canonical, history and Hopf geometry furnish a canonical, Lorentz-compatible $\mathbb{CP}^1$ spin-state bundle for a massive worldline, with no new coordinate and the correct Berry and $SU(2)$ holonomy — has this answer: the theory already contains exactly the right local Hopf and spin geometry, equivariantly identified with the rest space's spin group by Part I's own construction, with the canonical connection carrying the spin-$\tfrac12$ Berry normalization; the branch's real problem is proving that this geometry is the ubiquitous state bundle of massive matter, and that the full connection's pullback to it is undeformed Berry transport. **The local state-space and representation-geometric stage is frozen; the canonical local kinematic connection has been constructed and passes BMT; the dynamical identification stage begins here.** **The one-particle, on-shell Dirac representation stage is frozen here — the theory has reconstructed a candidate classical one-particle spinor kinematics and has not derived a quantum electron field.** The associated bundle reproduces the local spin state space, the Berry geometry, the Lorentz and Thomas transport, the BMT dynamics, and the complete free Dirac plane-wave spinor data — the $u$ and $v$ mode families with completeness, parameterized by future $p$ — without a canonical coordinate; a derivation of a quantum Dirac field is what remains, and it contains the physics: the matter-field occupation, an action principle, locality of the field equation, canonical anticommutation and statistics, the vacuum, and the physical particle–antiparticle reading. The next branch is not spin geometry; it is why matter on $B_{\rm rep}$ should be a quantized Dirac field valued in this bundle — where the theory would begin to earn the word "electron" rather than having reconstructed the electron's classical spinor kinematics. No further sphere, Hopf, quaternionic or Kaluza–Klein checks. The on-shell Dirac representation, its $v$ sector and the internal discrete-symmetry matrices are now on the constructed bundle (§§XX.8b, XX.9). **Program:** the occupation — a matter action on $B_{\rm rep}$ whose Dirac field is valued in the shell's spinor bundle, which is the one bridge left between the associated bundle and physical matter; the classification of meridian-dependent terms; and the field-theoretic form of $C$, $P$, $T$ with statistics. Everything after a failed arrow stops. The branch is insulated from Part XIX's triality and from the cosmological branches; it uses only the Kerr principle, the index–coordinate distinction and the shell's spin geometry.

## XX.12 A question the part raises: what spin becomes when the legs separate [S/O]

*Recorded after the freeze, as a question and not a result.* The author asks what quantum spin "becomes" once one passes from the aligned-legs regime of a pair to the regime in which the legs genuinely separate — the intuition being that the degree of freedom is repurposed while remaining rooted in the same object. The blow-up geometry of §XX.3 gives the intuition a precise form. At finite separation $Y = r\,\mathbf n$ carries a magnitude $r>0$ and a direction $\mathbf n$; ordinary geometry collapses everything at $Y = 0$, while the blow-up keeps the direction as the exceptional datum, $[Y]\in\mathbb{RP}^2$, oriented to $\mathbf n\in S(H_u)$, which by Part I is $\mathbb{CP}^1_{\rm shell}$. So the sense in which the original intuition survives is exact: **when the radial separation datum disappears, its directional datum can remain as the projective datum, and that residue has exactly the directional space that this part identifies with the candidate spin-state slot** — the occupation hypothesis staying open even here. The speculative reading is that what appears as an internal index in the coincident regime is the projectivized residue of a relational history degree of freedom that is geometrically resolved in the other — "spin is what the angular part of a relational degree of freedom looks like when its radial distinguishability has collapsed" — the polar-coordinate picture in three dimensions, in which the blow-up deliberately keeps $\theta$ at $r = 0$. That sentence is a possibility, not a theorem.

Two theories are possible, and the part does not choose. The conservative one: spin remains a local $\mathbb{CP}^1$ state at all separations — the shell supplies the slot independently of the history construction (§XX.4) — and finite separation *adds* relational information, $Y$ and spin being related but distinct. The radical one, which is the author's: the Bloch variable is the $r = 0$ boundary of a deeper finite-separation degree of freedom. Either way, separation need not turn spin into position; on the safe rule of §XX.6 — individual histories carry transport, comparisons of histories carry holonomy — it may *enrich* one local projective state into two resolved histories together with their comparative transport and relative phase $\Delta\gamma = \oint_C\mathcal A_H$, the first arising as the degenerate limit of the second. The part still refuses to claim that the two physical legs must be antipodal spin states.

**Theorem target.** A single state space $\mathcal X\to[0,\infty)$ with $\mathcal X_{r>0}$ the resolved doubled-history data and $\mathcal X_0 = \mathbb{CP}^1$, carrying a canonical connection whose restriction to $r = 0$ is the Berry connection of §XX.5 and whose restriction to $r>0$ is the comparative dual-history transport of Parts XIV–XV. That would make "quantum spin and resolved history separation are two regimes of one structure" a statement rather than a metaphor. **Kill tests:** the finite-separation theory must not force the two legs to remain antipodal spin states; it must reduce continuously to ordinary unitary spin dynamics as $r\to0$; it must preserve the Berry normalization; and it must say which observable makes $r$ physically distinguishable rather than merely projective. The mature form of the question is: *is quantum spin the projective boundary data of a deeper relational degree of freedom, whose radial component becomes physical only when the histories resolve?* **[S/O]**
